At the London Bankruptcy Court on Tuesday the case of Mr. J. Havelock Wilson, M.P. for lliddlesbrough, was again before the Registrar upon the adjourned hearing of an application for the approval of a com- position, accepted by the creditors, of 10s. in the pound. When the matter was before the Court on the 9th of April it was stated that i 2,685 was required to carry out the terms of the propaal. A sum of £5OO had been paid in, a furthe: £5OO would provided within a day or two, and the balance within a few weeks. Mr. Havelock Wilson applied for a further short adjournment, stating that since the last occasion a further sum of £9OO odd had beenhanded to the Official Receiver, and that within a short time the balance would be provided. Mr. Grey, the official recriver said £9OO had been ' received by him, £4OO having been paid in that day. There was also a cheque for £lOO, dated the 24th of May, which no doubt would be cleared. It was true that the last adjournment was to be final, but he had no desire to oppose a further one. The Registrar: How long do you suggest? Mr. Havelock Wilson: I think three weeks will be sufficient. The Official Receiver said that be did not oppose the application, as the alternative for creditors appeared to be 103. in the pound or nothing, and the registrar allowed the application to stand over for three weeks

THE; MILL BUILDING 80011.

The boom in mill building was referred to by Sir William Holland, who presided at a meeting of the Fine, Cotton Spinners and Doublers' Association in Man- chester on Tuesday. They could not shut their eyes, he declared, to the falt that if the building of new mills was continued long enough o:entually the supply must overtake the demand, and they must have a period of over-production of some classes of yarn. Being the largest buyers of fine cotton in the world, no one was so deeply interested in the question as that Association. Alluding to the unrest in the labwr market, he expressed the hope that with the reasonable spirit prevailing on both sides, the question would before long be satisfac- torily ended.

JOBBERY AT SaREWSBURY.

At a meeting of the Shrewsbury Town Council, on Monday, it was stated by the Chairman of the Water Committee that the Corporation had been systematic- ally defrauded by the coal contractor substituting some hundreds of tons of common coal for best srnokekss coal. The waterworks engineer, now dismissed, admitted that he had received from a coal contractor, now deceased, barrels of beer, bottles of whiskey, turkeys, and sums of money. Detection arose through a quarrel between two employees.

The Examiner, Satuiday, June 1, 1907 WOMAN'S REALM. !ALL RIGHTS 11115111V2D.j THE EXPLOITS OF THE ONE-EYED MAN.

The Forthcoming Royal Visit to Scotland.—The Prince and Princess of Wales will not be a great deal in London this season, and are to pay visits to the Duke ' and Duchess of Bac°touch, and the Duke and Duchess of Roxburgh° in Scotland in the summer. The Duke of Roxburghe's friendship with.the Prince of Wales is one of long standing, and he and the Duchess have spent a fortune in restoring Floors Castle, Kelso, in p epara- tion for the Royal guests. The Duchess, former y Miss May Goelet. is one of the three wealthy American heiress Duchesses of the present day, the others being the reigning Dechesses of Marlborough and Manchester. The Duke and DLit:tress of Buccleuott who are now in London, with their unmarried daughter, Lady Constance Scott, will entertain the Prince and Princess at Dalkeith Palace, their seat in Nlidlothian which was rlaced at the disposal of the Eing and Queen on the occasion of the Royal visit to Edinburgh, after His Majesty's accession. The Duke of Buccleuch holds one of the proudest positions in the peerage, and is not only the second largest landowner in Scotland, but also one of the wealthiest of the dukes. The Original " Little Jack Horner." —Miss Katherine Horner, who is to marry Mr. Raymond Asquith, comes of a very old Somerset family. She is the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Horner, of Alerts Park, which origin- ally formed part of of the monastery lands of Glaston- bury Abbey. When the monasteries fell upon evil days John Horner who was steward of the temporalities of the abbey. managed to secure for him self the title deeds jof the Monor of Xells, and he was t he original "little Jack Hornet" of nursery lore, who put in his finger and pulled out a plum. That plum has b een in the possession of the Horner family for nearly five hundred years, and it has Wirer been the eustom for the eldest son in the family to be named John. Mr. Raymond Asquith, who promises to emulate the brilliant career of his father, I the Chancellor of the Exchequer, is following Mr. ' Herbert Gladstone's example in taking a Somerset bride. Mrs. Gladstone is the daughter of Sir Richard Paget, and there is a relationship between the Pagets of Somerset and the Homers of Melts Park. Fifty Years of Literary Work.—The many friends of Miss Betham-Edwards, desire to call attention to the jubilee testimonial which is being got up for this well- known authoress, who has completed fifty years of 1 literary work. Miss Bath-am-Edwards is a native of Suffolk, and made her first venture into the field of literature whilst still a country girl. That was in 1856, when Charles Dickens gave £5 for a poem "The Golden Bee" which appeared in "household Words." Her ever favourite story " Kitty" is just appearing in a eixpenny edition, and many of her novels have had a steady sale for decades, whilst her poems are still often recited in public. She is recognised authority on agri- culture, whilst another favourite subject is French country life, and her latest book is entitled " Literary Rambles in France." Latterly health and strength have failed Miss Betham-Edwards, and it is hoped that the proposed tribute will meet with hearty and generous support. Many well-known names are on the committ se, including the Lord Mayor of London, Lord Brassey, Lord Courtney, and the Bishop of Ripon. Women as Motor-Car Drivers.—Recent accidents have drawn attention to the handling of motor-cars by women. It becomes tedious always to look on as a passenger, and many women hanker after the excitement of driving and controlling a car. Much however has been said to the effect that women are less endowed than men, with the nerve or power to act with c oolness and good judgment in sudden danger, and therefore , they should only be allowed to drive low power cars„ incapable a high speeds. One is struck by such an argument, because it implies that only men should be permitted to driYeat such a speed as to take the turning ' on one wheel, but in cases of such recklessness, avoid- ance of accident depends as much upon luck as good management. In nineteen cases out of twenty it is the high speed that makes the accident unavoidable, no matter who the driver may be. Women as a rule, drive with more consideration for other users of the road,and many display a remarkable aptitude in the handling of cars. _ . .

Dearer Drapery and Paper Clothes.—The much higher cost of tussore and Japanese silks this season is only symptomatic of a general rise in the prices of dress materials, and in fact all staple drapery goods. The rise, we are told, is not as yet so marked as it will be later on, when drapers have to renew stocks at increased wholesale prices, but it already affects many necessi- ties, and ordinary silk lining that could be had for less than 2s. a yard, is now difficult to obtain at 2s. 6i. Silk fabrics of every description are much dearer, and the, same remark applies to ribbohs, lace, and also to all linen and woollen dress materials. It is said that we shall soon be able to buy dress materials woven of paper thread and yarn, and if they are as durable and service- able as we are given to understand, it is to these that we shall have to turn for our requirements. So many wonderful things have been done with paper, and it is stated that it can be woven into armost every conceiv- able fabric, presenting even better wearing properties than the textile materials of ordinary use. Hess to Arrange Stripes.—Leading dressmakers are employing very iittfe in thway- orte;enr.;agn whea making up striped gowns,they prefer instead to arrange the stripes in different directions. For instance a skirt cut with the stripes running perpendicularly, may have a flounce, a hem, or striping's cut on -the cross. Many variations are thus pessibleput it should be remem- bered that only in indeterminate colours is the idea likely to be a success. In black and white for example, the scheme is apt to be particularly trying, having a very uncomfortable effect on the eyes, for first the feel swimming, and then they can only see bits of such a "creation "at one time. The New Prints.—Simple prints are being chosed for young girls in preference to linen for morning wear. The new prints are in the prettiest old-world colours and patterns, and are usually made up into a full bell skirt with draped fichn bodice gathered into a belt. Shoulder epaulets of the 'print are inserted in the bodice, undersleoves of the bishop persuasion, also 'collar and vest of lawn completing this dainty costume. Grey for Evening Wear.—ln selecting grey for even- ing wear great care should be exercised,for many shades that in the daytime appear charming have an undesirable way of looking by artificial light very much like white that has become grimy with old age. Grey for night wear must be very grey, not pale, and a sort of dull cloud hue makes up very charmingly, especially in tulle and chiffon. A Curious Ornament.—Flexible snakes are very much in vogue just now, either coiled in or out the hair, round the neck or about the upper part of the arm. I These snakes are of gold, and usually very long, and the beads are studded with precious stones. When worn in the hair, the head rests almost on the forehead, and the I utmost one can say in favour of the mode is, that in effect it makes for the carious and weird. Concerning Hammocks.—Hammocks this year are being made in coloured twine, beautifully blended with Oriental stripes, while some of the best show the sides ornamented with pink or red roses and foliage, all made in coloured twine. Slung beneath the trees, these dainty hammocks will look ,particularly charming, and much more picturesquelthan the more costly varieties which are built of soft stout canvas with a pillow of the same securely fixed at one end. The hammock of natural coloured twine is little in evidence this season. in shopland, and all such stock will probably be sold off at a considerable reduction.

BY EDWARD TOWLER.

VI.—THE PERIL OF THE SUBMARINE. When the Submarine lay in seclusion in the Essex marshes on the upper reaches of the Blackwater, it was thought by the One-eyed Man to be immune from capture; indeed, the latter contingency had never entered into his calculations at all. Possessing a speed of some thirty-five knots an hour under water, the eraft had yet to be built that conld in any way approach the Submarine, and a dive would always take them out of harm's way. But it must not be thought for a moment that Inspector Bullen or Scotlaud Yard had either forgotten or ignored the Submarine. Orders had been given secretly to the Navy to 'signal every Submarine to stop until her identity was known.

A reward of one thousand pounds which had been offered for the capture of the One- eyed Man's Submarine was an inducement to every A.B. in the fleet cruising in home waters to keep his eyes -skinned; but up to the present no such luck as a glimpse of her "whale back" had shewn above the surface to cheer the eyes of those to whom one thou- sand pounds was a life competency. One memorable day, the whole gang, with the exception of the Greaser, who was at Truro awaiting orders, were aboard the Sub- marine, ready for a coup at a mansion on the shores of St. Austell's Bay, in which was stored some priceless old gold plate, brought over in the days of Drake from the galleon Santa Anna by an ancestor of the owner, who boarded and cut out the galleon in Southern seas.

This, then, was where the One-eyed Man was bound for when the following adventure overtook him and his satellites. They had passed the estuary of the Crouch, and Foulness Point was astern. Crossing into the West Swin, they passed the Mouse Light on the part side and made past the Girdlet Light into the Prince's Channel. They had come up for an observation, and the night lay dark. Ahead a light, flashing red and white, shewed brilliantly.

"What is it? asked the One-eyed Man, fcr he was no mariner, Breaci being skipper in charge. The latter took out his watch, timing the flash. "It's the Tongue Light," he said, briefly. "We are off Queen's Channel and shall make the Foreland very soon now." "Great Cesar! What's that?"

He might well ejaculate, for a brilliant beam of light cut the heavens like a mighty sword of silver, and then, like an avenging finger from the heavens, swept round and deluged them in its brilliance. "Below with you!" yelled Bresei. "Below all! Simini, stand by! It's a cursed cruiser! We are seen!" • It was as he had said, for the cruiser Merionethshire, going through a night attack drill, had picked them up in the focns of her searchlight, and their figures stood out plain as a pikestaff upon the whale back of the Submarine.

The gang scurried below, bolting like rab- bits for their burrow, but too late. The Merionethshire had sharp eyes aboard her, and as the searchlight picked them out of the blackness of the night, so surely were they seen.

In an instant the cruiser sprang to life, and the voice of the officer rang out: "It's the Submarine of that villainous gang. Load the forward gun!" A dick or two, and the sharp snap of a breach. "Ready, sir." "Throw a shot across her bows! Fire! "_

A column of water flashed up fifty yards ahead of the stationary Submarine. "Why the dickens doesn't he sink?" cried the officer to his captain, who had joined him on deck.

Aye, why didn't they? That was what Bresci and Simini wanted to know, for the ballast tanks would not fill.

"Go ahead, sir," said Bimini to Bresci. "Go ahead, or they'll plump cold shot into us here, and we shall sink with all our boodle."

Bresct turned the lever over and the Sub- marine shot forward, shewing two feet of free hoard to the Merionethshire's guns. It was a toes up now, for even as she forged ahead there came a flash and a weird whistle, as a common shell tore by the flying,boat. The speed of the Submarine was some twenty knots an hour—half her usual speed— for whatever had gone wrong with the sinking apparatus had evidently thrown something connected with the starboard engine out of gear. Only one of the cylinders could be worked, and in this crippled condition she tore through the rising seas, with the cruiser in pursuit, a mile and a-half in the rear. The One-eyed Man's only hope was that they, on the cruiser, knowing the immense value of the booty on board, would not try to sink her, but capture her and her crew. In this he read aright, for the captain- of the Merionethshire gave the order not to sink her. He was hoping the firing would attract the attention of others of the Channel Squad- ron, and that the Submarine would be hemmed in and taken. He judged that some- thing had gone wrong aboard her, or she would have sunk long since. His own speed was twenty-three knots an hour, and under forced draught he could develop twenty-four and a-half, so he hoped to keep her in sight long enough to attain his object of capture. On board the Sujamarine the sliding shutter of the hatch had been opened, and a man kept watch, lashed to the short stan- chion, Ao which the flagstaff was usually fixed. From this stanchion ran a small telephone attachment to the interior, so that constant communication could he kept up with those inside by the lookout. Ahead, a light shewed every half-minute across the tumbling waters. The lookout tele- phoned down, "North Foreland light, sir, on the starboard bow."

"Aye, aye," came the reply. "How does the cruiser bear?"

"About two miles astern, sir. They don't :want to hit us." "Report any craft you see immediately!" "Aye, aye, sir." The Submarine tore on, picking up the Nor' Sand's head shortly after. _ _

The cruiser redo-übled her efforts, and her shbt dropped round, ahead and astern of the Submarine, shewing the perfect practice made, and how easy for them to have sunk her had they wished. Rapidly nearing Dover and its attendant dangers, the One-eyed Man himself determined to go above; he could stand being below no longer. Upon his hard. set face no emotion shewed, but, icy as the mask was, a raging fear consumed him. Was this to be the end of his labours, the end of his schemes? Were all his dastardly crimes to be brought to nought? Well, it looked so. Going up to the lookout he said, briefly: "What is that light flashing ahead on the starboard bow?"

"The South Foreland, sir, two flashing electric— There's Dover just ahead; that'll *wake 'em up there." The dull boom from the cruiser shook the night air as he spoke. "Au, see, see, they've done it, by Heaven! We're dead meat! That's the torpedo flotilla coming out!" The One-eyed Man ran below. • "Shake her up, Bresci. Will she go?" he asked. "Soon, Boss, soon. We've located the de- fect; the exhaust had jammed." " How much longer can we reckon on? The cruiser has wakened up the Dover torpedo flotilla with her firing, and they are nosing round like dogs. It is a question of minutes only before we are surrounded. We cannot remain hidden from them long." Tinkle, tinkle, went the telephone bell. " Well? " queried the One-eyed Man. "The cruiser has put her .searchlight on us, sir, and these confounded wasps have picked us up. She'll fire no more now, for fear of hitting them. You had better come up.,, You hear, Bresci ?" he asked. . "Yes, Boss," said the Italian. "Hold 'em off. Parley, pretend to surrender; anything for ten minutes, then we can sink. Send the lookout below, and when we signal, drop be- low yourself, snap the scuttle, for when I ring we shall be all- clear."

The One-eyecl Man stepped on deck. "Go below," he said, briefly, to the other, and the man stepped down. Gazing ahead. 1e saw

The volume is published by Messrs. Chapma* and Hall.

the advancing lights • of some half-dozen tor- pedo-boats. He himself was in the glare of the cruiser's searchlight, that, dodge as he might, picked him up wiith an almost miraculous ease. Fife minutes more and the leading torpedo-boat was within hailing dis- tance.

"Surrender, or we ram you!" shouted the officer on the bridge of the dancing craft, through a megaphone. "Who are you? Speak, quick!" ' The One-eyed Man was a wolf at bay,. Even now he might escape, and he listened with straining ears for the signal from below. "Who are you?" was repeated from the torpedo-boat. Find out, and be shot to pia!" said the One-eyed Man. "Do you surrender?" cried the other, now only some two hundred yards off. "Aye, aye," said the One-eyed Man, "I surrender. It is all up. Stand by." The Submarine slowed up and her engines stopped. .

ilow goes it, Bresci? Are we clear?" Below they were working like maniacs, for the neck of every man was in a halter, and they knew it. "We can couple up in two, minutes. Get one of 'em aboard; we'll sink the swine for giving us this fright. Tell him to send a boat."

The One-eyed Man sprang on deck. "I sur- render," he cried. "Send a boat." A hand was waved from the torpedo-boat and a boat lowered. An officer and a dozen men jumped in and pulled off to the Sub- marine.

The Merionethshire, to whom all this was due, lay off mothering the tiny craft, like a hen mothers chickens. The boat reached the side of the Submarine; an A.B. jabbed a boat-hook into a ring on the plating, and she lay rising and falling by the side of the other. At that moment the tinkle sounded, and to the One-eyed Man it was the sweetest music he had ever heard. Never had he been so helpless. Schemes he could invent and carry out, criminal problems solve, but here he had been helpless indeed, and it had galled him intensely to be so entirely in the hands of his subordinates. However, it was ended now.

, The officer and two men sprang aboard. "Do you surrender quietly, or is it to be force?" he asked, fingering a huge Navy revolver, while the two men silently covered the One-eyed Man with their own weapons. " Pax ! ' said the One-eyed Man, with a "But come below."

"If there is any treachery, mind-" began the officer. "Treachery!" said the One-eyed Man. "My dear sir, my machinery is all wrong orl should have sunk long ago. I have only a tirew of four men, and here you have a dozen hanging like limpets to my boat; but if you are afraid-" "Afraid!" said the lieutenant, briefly. "Follow me, men. Lead me to your cabin," he commanded, addressing the One-eyed Man. "Kindly drop your arms, if you have any." "I have none Lieutenant; follow me." .

He stepped below and the lieutenant followed down the scuttle. The moment he did so, and his feet were upon the first rungs of the lad- der, he was seized and dragged down, feet foremost, coming down the stair full run, as the steel cover snapped like the breach of a gun into its place, and at the same moment the boat-hook was torn from the hand of the leading seaman, who was holding on, and wtruggling in the water alongside were the two A.B.'s who had stood by their officer on the Submarine's deck. The latter, now some twenty fathoms deep, was going her usual thirty-five knots. When the lieutenant had recovered himself! he found his pistol had been whipped out of his hand and his sword removed from his belt. The Navy man was frothing at the mouth. " Treachery ! " he said. "I thought you had broken down, or you would never have got me here like this." The One-eyed Man smiled. My dear sir, we were broken down, but my engineers Luckily, at the psychological moment, put the boat to rights. Now, if you will name the point you would like to be landed at, we will do our best to oblige you. Having but lately escaped ourselves, we are merciful, otherwise —well, it is not particularly healthy for those who are not of us to visit UB."

An hour later, a lieutenant of the Navy was dumped ashore a trifle to the south'ard of Dungeness, with no other harm than the loss pf a certain amount of dignity. In that he was more than usually lucky. "I hope," he said, "we meet again. By Heavens I do! Next time-well, you look out, that's all! "

The One-eyed Man smiled. "I beg you not to cross or attempt to cross my path," he said.. "I do not spare twice." . . .

But both were io meet again, though they new it not, and that was to be his Waterloo for one of them.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

A PURITANICAL QUEEN.

People who are acquainted with the habits of the Queen of Denmark are wondering, writes a oorrespondent, how far she will be able to ac- commojate herself to the, less rigid regime of English society. When at home, her Majesty guides her conduct on the most severe principles, and never plays cards or visits race meetings. It is only when her attendance is absolutely neces- sary that she even attends balls, theatres, or similar light entertainments. The result is that the Danish Court is about the most dull and de- corous in Europe, and it is just possible that her Majesty will not be able to appreciate the fes- tivities that are being prepared in her honour during her visit to this country. Her Majesty is, nevertheless, one of the wealthiest of Royal per- sonages, and brought her husband an immense fortune. Her jewels—as fashionable London will soon have an opportunity of judging—are among the finest owned Lty any lady in Europe or America. The King of Denmark, though he has of late years acquired a lot of his wife's serious- ness of manner, is a very keen sportsman, and is likely to enjoy very thoroughlf his visit to Ascot.

COLOURED FOOTGEAR.

The rapidly encroaching advances of _coloured footgear cannot for a moment be ignored, - says the Bystander. The determination that this shall be is very marked, and enhanced, both in the matter of excellence and prices, by those who have from the first pioneered the notion. As an accompaniment to the coloured summer frock, washing or otherwise, the belief is crystallising seriously that the souliers en suite are an im- perative need. And be it also speedily known, ,only the quietest, most unaggressive shapes are 'accounted bon ton. For coloured shoes, the big flap and Brobdingnagian buckles are the last de- tails recognised by the elect. Then, too, a largo predilection reigns in favour of delicately. coloured cloth tops, fitted to a patent-leather golosh. These, although by no means new, have acquired a character under tho present prevail- ing atmosphere of approval they have never be- fore possessed. Reverting to shoes, quite extra- ordinary colours, such as raspberry red, burgun- dies, royal blue, and leaf green are accepted with the greatest aplomb, and, keeping well in step with 'their confreres, the hosiers are in a position to meet practically all demands. CHAMPAGNE-COLOURED COSTUME.

Quite the latest and smartest type is the charming costume which forme the subject of our illustration. The material in which it is realised is a fine smooth cloth of the most deli- cate and becoming tone of champagne, an ex- ceedingly fashionable nuance just now. The upper part of the costume is a cross between a pelerine and a bolero, and has loose banging fronts, from which turn back two wide revers, stitched at the edge, and ornamented with large

hmids,4me buttons'. Between these fronts comes a delightful little waistcoat of Eastern embroidery, carried out in various shades of Chinese-blue silk. The waistcoat crosses in front, and is beaded with a. narrow band of stitched cloth. The sleeves are of the popular "kimono" type, and are composed of overlapping bands of cloth, adorned with lines of machining at the edge. The skirt is of the pleated variety, wide stitched box-pteats being alternated by pointed and buttoned straps, from which spring wide plain tucks.- This skirt looks equally well in house or walking length, and is quite unusually smart in effect.

THE CHAPERON RECALLED.

A notable circumstance about the present sea- son is the reappearance of the chaperon, says Vanity Fair. On one famous ,occasion last sum- mer all the young men asd maidens surreptiti- ous!y deserted the dancing-room, and were sub- sequ,nitly discovered enjoying the cool night broezes on the roof. What became of the dainty chiffons and satins in those sooty regions can better be ;magined than described. It was felt that the fast vanishing chaperon had her uses after all.

TO-DAY'S RECIPK.

SALADE DE TOMATES FARCIES.--ChOOSS , even- sized tomatoes, cut a hole in the top of each, and remove the seeds and some of the interior. Place on ice to keep firm, and in the meantime mince any remains of chicken or Areal finely, add a npoontut ot minced tongue and a- few small cooked new potatoes passed through a sieve. Moisten with two or three spoonfuls of rich mayonnaise sauce. Fill the tomatoes with this farce, replace the tops, and serve on a bed of !ettuee.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
SMALL, BUT CONSPICUOUS

Which is the most conspicuous ingredient of the English wedding cake? Surely the currant. Mince pies depend almost entirely upon currants for their flavour and richness, so do Eccles Cakes and Banbury Cakes and scores of other tea-table dainties.

Many millions of pounds weight of currants are used annually in the making of bread, and the demand for this most wholesome and nutritive food is still on the increase.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was a hood attenciance at an open meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A.. on Sunday. The Mayor presided and speaking about "Loving kindness" his Worship said it was their duty, as well as a great privilege, to show kindness day by day especially in the home. What power or influence had a man over his fellows, who was not kind athome ? Charity certainly began at home, but should not stay there. He had often been struck by the kind acts-he had seen in the streets of Warrington, such as boys running to a blind man to guide him across the street, so that he might be kept out of danger. He liked to see and hear of men being kind to their wives and families, and hoped the women _present would always show kindness to their husbands who oftentimes came home after an anxious and disappointing day, and who -needed sympathy. (Applause.) Mr. T. Harrison, of Oldham, gave a, humorous address, dealing with his personal experi- ences in life. The Rev. John Yonge and Mr. G. Wallington also took part in the meeting. Mr. Yonge said that was the first time they had had the Mayor in the chair, and they hoped to have him as their chairman again, tither as Mayor or in his private capacity. , Mr. E. Pearce sang "The Lord is my Shepherd" and "Arm ye Brave," in an effective manner.

FEDERATION IN AMERICA.

lar L i 4 • aw says that fed4ration Of Churches eeded far more in America than Britain.

The great social, moral, civic, aria rerzgloas problems which are so intensely real in all the great towns and cities over there never can be solved except by the innumerable Churches of all the Evangelical denomina- tions becoming one solid organised force. That need in no way interfere with the Churches and the denominations doing their own particular work. It is, I am convinced, by this real and living compact federation of the Churches in every district of the Stated that they will be able to deal effectively with Tammany in all its forms, with the saloon, impurity, gambling, and other forces of iniquity which are eating at the heart of the great centres of populations—and, indeed, throughout the country—and I believe it is only in this way it can be done."

FREE CHURCH SUMMER SCHOOL.

One of the most important features of Dr. Harris' Presidential year in connection with the Free Church Council is to be the Summer School at Cambridge, from July 29 to August 10. In this month's F rce Church Chronicle Dr. Harris gives some particulars of what is to be attempted at. Cambridge. Some of the greatest " stars " of the theological world are to assist.. Lectures are to be held in • the mornings and evenings, and a most varied programme has. been prepared. The. after- noon rambles will be times of the, greatest int,crerst. Excursions will be made to places in and around Cambridge, Special attention being given to spots notable for their religious his- tory. In order to allow as large a number as possible attending the school, the inclu- sive fee for ten days has been fixed as low as £3 15s. (covering bed, breakfast, luncheon, dinner, etc.). In order for arrangements to be completed, it is necessary, that early appli- cation be made; in any case. June 30 is the latest date upon which members can be en- rolled. Full information can be . obtained from the local secretaries of the School, 11, Sydney-street, Cambr:dge.

PRACM SUNDAY

The Nationai Free Church Council has made fill appeal to all the Free Churches to observe Sunday, Rine 9, as Peace Sunday. itia view ofl the assembling at. The Hague the !following week of the ,International Pc-ace Conference. It is suggested that .speial sermons should be preabhed and prayers offered on behalf of the success of the movement, and in par- ticular in fyour of +.ll proposition to intro- duce mediation before war.

FREE CHURCH CONVENTION.

For several year; past the National Free Church Council has conducted summer gather- ings at different centres for the deepening of spiritual life. This affords opportunity for Free Church people in the holiday season to hear some of the leading preachers of the day and to combine physical with spiritual refresh- ment. This year a Convention is to be held at Aberystwyth from June 10th to 13th. Dr. Rendel Harris will preside, and will give some of his unique talks. In addition there will be Revs. Thomas Phillips 8.A., Thomas Law, J. Puleston Jones, M.A., T. Nightingale, and other English and Welsh ministers, besides Gipsy Smith, Mr. Silas K. Hocking', and Mr. J. Compton Rickett, M.P., &c. Delegates from the whole of Wales are being appointed to attend these gathering, but special tickets can imr obtained on application to the National Offices, Memorial Hall, Londonl E.C. E. M.

The Examiner, SP turclav5 June 1 1907.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. Annual Nesting of the Warringtoil and District League.

, The annual meeting of the Warrington and District I Association Football League was held at the Marquis of Granby Inn, on Thursday evening. Mr. W. H. Smalley presided over a good attendance. ' In his annual report the HON. SECRETARY (Mr. C. T. Cox) pointed out that Oughtrington Park had again won the Ist Division Championship with 30 points and : Castner Kehler Reserve and Widnes St. Paul's had tied • for the 2nd Division Championship with 34 points each. Oughtrington Park had won the championship with a larger majority than in the previous season. With regard to the 2nd Division it had been decided that the two teams should play off on a ground selected by the League Committee on the first Saturday in September, the proceeds of the gate to go to the League fund after paying expenses. At the commencement of the season they had 11 teams in the Ist Division and 15 in the 2nd. He was sorry to say that it was still very difficult to get some of the clubs to fulfil their League engagements and he would like to suggest that for the coming season the Conference meeting be held on June 13th. so that if necessary they could call another meeting later to fix up all fixtures before the season opens, as he thought that was where the trouble lay. It was the policy of all club secretaries to fill in the fixtures as early as possible so that if the unexpected happened they had the latter part of the season to fall back upon. In con- clusion Mr. Cox thanked the Club Secretaries for their assistance during the year, and Mr. and Mrs. F. ,J. Whittle for their hospitality. The balance sheet showed that the year closed with a balance in hand of £4 12s. lid.

The report and statement of accounts were unani- mously. approved. The appointment of officers was then proceeded with Mr. James Dodds was unanimously re-elected president. The other officers were also re-elected and were : —Mr. W. H. Smalley, chairman, Mr. C. T. Cox, secretary, and Mr. H. Hawthorn treasurer. At the conclusion of the business me.ting an enjoy- able smoker was held, Mr. Smalley again presiding. Mr. James Dodds who was prevented by illness from attending wrote: "I am sorry that after all I cannot be with you to-night. Although I am in much better health than for several months past, I have to be very careful, and it would be unwise to venture out in the night air in this east wind. Had the weather been warm and genial, as on the eve of June it ought to have been, nothing would have prevented my being with you. By next winter I hope to be quite fit and to witness some of the League matches. May I ask you to convey my congratulations to the winners of the shield. I have known the Oughtrington Park Club for a very long time now,and I believe they play not only a very good game but a fair and sportsmanlike one, and I wish them all success in future, while hoping that the other clubs in the League will strive not only to win the shield, but to raise the character and quality of the play, so as to render it imperative that the Association game shall have the relative pro- minence in Warrington that it basin the more important Lancashire towns."

===== 0002601_19070601_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION OF THE DODDS SIIIRLD.

Mr. W. H. SMALLAY, in the absence of the president, handed the handsome silver challenge shield, presented by Mr. Dodds, to the Oughtrington Park team the holders for the first year together with gold centre medals as League champions. He was delighted to have been chairman of the league during the first year in which the shield had been offered, and he was sure that it was the finest ever offered for competition in IWarrington in connection with any sport. (Rear,hear). He noticed that a prominent Warringtonian and a Northern Rugby Union enthusiast, said the Association game was coming forward to such an extent that they had better be doing something for fear the Rugby game should be broken down. He (Mr. Smalley) thought that in a town like Warrington there was ample room for both codes, and whilst he did not want to see Rugby go down he would like to see Association come forward. (Hear, hear.) He hoped that the success of the Oughtrington Park team would lead to other Cheshire clubs coming to join the League, and he was sure that they could not have a better inducement to do so than was offered in the handsome shield Mr. DOdds had presented to the League. (Applause). Mr.CßANTLEY,secretary of the Oughtrington Club, said he did not think that next season their team would be quite so strong, and therefore other clubs would have an opportunity of winning the shield. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WHITTLE said there was a singular appropriate- ness in the success of the Oughtrington team, RS Mr. Dodds for many years lived at Oughtrington. He was sure that Mr. Dodds would wish him to heartily con- gratulated the Park players on their success.*(Applause.) Mr. CRITCHLEY pointed out that in 1904-5 the Park team won both the League and the Cup competition, whilst last season they were runners up for the cup, and captured the League. He thought the medals which accompanied the shield rather disgraced Mr. Dodds' handsome present, and he hoped that next year the Cora- ..satra.. of lila LAnzue would see their way to give gold medals. (Hear, hear). Mr. SMALLEY said that the Committee were also boping that they would shortly be able to give gold medals.

-0i:1;;"r congratulatory speeches were also made, and during tbe evening the company was ably entertained songs being given by Messrs. Foulkes, Moorlield, Hawthorn and Evans.

THE BAPTIST UNION.

The spring meetings of the above union were held at the Milton Baptist Church,Action Bridge on Thursday. Owing to the unfortunate climatic conditions the attendances were not so large as they otherwise would have been. The proceedings commenced with a meet- ing of committee at which it was decided that the next meetings be held at the church at ,Hill Cliff e, on Thursday, September 26th. This meeting over, a Conference was held in the chapel, where, after a short time had been spent in prayer, a paper was read by Mr. J. T. Beswick, of Golborne, the topic chosen by Mr. Beswick being the practicability, or otherwise, of "the district church." The paper was a continuance of a subject introduced at the last meeting by Rev. J. S. Hughes who argued for the establishment of" the district church" in place of the numerous various churches of the denomination, which while they are conspicous for their independency, are often con- spicuous by their effort to maintain their existence. The lines upon which Mr. Beswick travelled were con- trary to those advanced by the reader of the previous paper and in the course of his paper he endeavoured to show the many impracticabilties which surrounded the scheme, and contended that by maintaining their inde- pendence each individual church would be more healthy and vigourous and would be capable of greater and more successful efforts. Discussion followed led off by Mr. Hume, Little Leigh, who in the main was in agreement with the spirit of the paper. The discussion was continued, Mr. E. Lloyd Jones and Rev. J. F. Matthews, taking up an opposite view of the subject and contending that the question was one which event- ually would have to be taken into serious consideration and asserting that the difficulties raised were mostly imaginary and could easily be reckoned with. Following this meeting, tea was served in the school- room to which a very fair number of friends sat down. A public meeting held in the evening was presided over by. the President, Rev. J. F. Matthews, who delivered a brief address in his usual forceful and inspiriting manner. The visiting speaker was Rev. W. G. Pope of Toxteth Tabernacle, Liverpool, who delivered a most powerful and impressive address on the subject of his ideal Church, the conspicuous , elements of which must be spirituality, progressiveness, and aggressiveness. So deep an impression was created by Mr. Pope's utteranc,as, that the succeeding speaker, Mr. E. Lloyd Jones, requested the Chairman to devote the remainder of the meeting to prayer, in which sue- gestion the meeting acquiesced, and a memorable meeting was thus brought to a close.

• The British steamer Boniface, from Galveston for Liverpool with a cargo of cotton, arrived at Bermuda on Thursday with her cargo afire and decks crumbling. A doctor at a St. Helens inquest anr Thursday said many sudden deaths had been recorded of late, cases where the thymus gland was swollen. A resolution demanding disestablishment and dis- endowment as a preliminary to union with the Church ' of Scotland was passed by ahuge majority at the United Free Church Assembly on Thursday.

Speaking at a Nationalist meeting in Pettigo, county Fermanagh, on Thursday, Mr. J. C. Dolan, M.P., said he looked forward to the day when the Union Jack would be dragged in the mud awl the sons of Erin would march victorious with the green flag waving over them.

Tali SHIP CANAL 3ILL.

0??1331f1J3 FROM WARRINGTON ----- Salt Wat3r ail L 9331 Maatifactories

The Manchester Ship Canil Bill, which has already passed the House of Lords, was on Tuesday considered by a committee of the House of Commons. The bill empowers the company, among other things, to pump an additional quantity of water from the estuary of the Mersey into the section from Eastham to Latchford, and thence into the sections above, for the purpose of locking vessels up and down the canal. To this pro- posal opposition is again offered by the Corporation of Warrington and by Messrs. Crostleld and Sons, soap and chemical manufacturers' Messrs. Fairclough and Sons, Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co., Limited, Messrs. Rylands Bros. the Whitecross Company, and !Messrs. Pearson.. and Knowles, Limited. The opponents no longer con- tend that there is no immediate necessity for an addi- tional supply of water for the canal, and the pro motors, on their part,have conceded a clause granting protection to VVarrington in regard to any further abstraction or diversion of water from the upper reaches of the Mersey. The only question at issue now is whether, as a result of the proposed punsping,such an extra quantity of salt-bearing water will be brought into the river in the neighbourhood of the works of the petitioning traders as will prejudicially affect the "feed" of their boilers.

It was stated before the Lords Committee, Mr. Balfour Browne, LC., said,that the Canal Company had to put water into the Mersey at Walton sluices. This statement was not challenged at the time. Since then, however, this action had been brought for the purposes, as he said, of the opposition to the bill, and when last Saturday the Company sought to open the sluices the Corporation of Warrington had men there to stn. them. The proceedings of the Company in attempting to open the sluices, he maintained, was thoroughly justified. Mr. Balfour Browne further said that at the time the Ship Canal was made Manchester was in low water, but it had been booming ever since the work was corn- .pleted. Manchester had become one of the most important ports in the kingdom, but the traffic bad beoome so great it was necessary to replace water lost by vessels locking out. Already the pinch was being felt. The amount of water lost in locking out vessels, some of which were 500 ft. long with 58! t. beam, with a capacity of 10,000 tons, was enormous, and unless there were some means adopted of replacing the lost water a serious state of affairs would be brought about. The opposition to the bill was the same as that in the House of Lords, and came from the Warrington Cur pomtion and the traders of Warrington, who feared that the pumping of water would deplete the river Mersey. Sir George Nares, the acting Commissioner, was satisfied that no danger to the ohanael existed, and did not oppose. Mr. W. H. Hunter, chief manager to the company, gave evidence. He explained that theadditional supply of water was made necessary by the increase in the traffic of the canal, the greater size of the ships using the waterway, and the necessity for lucking vessels up and down the canal without any delay. The most reasonable, practicable, and efficient way of making this provision was by pumping water into theseveral lengths of the canal. At neap tides a great many of the Liver- pool docks were now supplied with water pumped from the estuary. The pumps would never be used except when the water was absolutely required for lockage purposes. He estimated that during the first ten years the pumps would not be used more than five times in eaoh year, and it would only be as the tragic grew that their use would be increased. Referring to the objection of the Warrington traders, the witness said he did not believe there would be the increased salinity at Rixton Junction which they feared, because the salt water would have to and its way th9re against a more powerful stream of fresh water. But in order to make the possibility even more probable, he proposed so to place the pumps at Latchford that the suction would be only six feet below the surface of the water, and thus the water drawn would contain a minimum quantity of salt. The committee adjourned. A WRIT ISSUED.

In the action of the Manchester Ship Canal Company v. the Corporation of Warrington, counsel stated on Tuesday before Jotice Kekewich, in London Chancery Division, that a writ had been issued in the action, and he asked leave to serve notice of motion for Friday next for an interim injunction. Justice Kekewich gave the requisite leave. WEDNESDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

The House of Commons Committee continued the consideration of the Bill on Wednesday when further evidence was given in support of the proposal of the Canal Company to pump an additional supply of water from the Mersey estuary into the waterway for lockage Mr. Hunter, the chief engineer, was cross-examined by counsel on behalf of the Warrington Corporation and the traders whose works are on the banks of the Mersey. He would not admit that the result of his pro- posal would be to bring into the river in the neighbour- hood of these works such an amount of salt-bearing water as would prejudicially affect their boilers. But even if it were otherwise,he was not prepared to advise the company to accept the compensation clause for which the petitioner asked. Replying to the Chairman, he explained that powers were taken in the Bill to erect pumps at all the locks, but it was not intended at the present time to erect them at Irlam.

Sir A. Kennedy, president of the Institution of Civil Engineers, who for the purposes of this inquiry had examined the sections of the Canal at Warrington, said he had come to the conclusion that the salinity of the water passing from the Canal into the river at Rixton Junction would not be appreciably increased as the result of the pumping proposed under the Bill. The Committee adjourned.

!of water at low water of spring tides in the said : channel." It is admitted that on any reading the Ship Canal Company have not fulfilled their duty, but a dis- pute has arisen as to the true meaning of these words. The plaintiffs is this action say the words mean that I the channel must be dredged so that, whenever there is 1 low water of spring tides, there shall be a depth of eight I feet of water in the channel. It seems to me that this is I tir.l true meaning. Mr. Justice Byrne so held, and made la declaration to that effect. The Court of Appeal, how- ever, though it expressed concurrence with Mr. Justice Byrne's conclusions, so varied his order as largely to destroy its effect. It was argued there for the Ship Canal Company, as before your lordships, that in reality a depth of eight feet of water at low water of spring tides meant a depth of eight feet below low water at Warrington Bridge, which was, and, as the defendants maintain, still is, ten feet above Ordnance datum. Beginning with that assumption, the defendants further argued that the channel,though not at the time of action brought deep enough to comply with the statutory requirements, had nevertheless at some earlier date been dredged deep enough to satisfy the Warrington Bridge ' measure. Thereupon the Court of Appeal expunged the ' declaration of 11fr. Justice Byrne, aad, in place of it, fetid that

• THE ORIGINAL OBLIGATION to dredge had been complied with at some date prior to • January 1, 1897, and declared that " the obligation of the Aanchester Ship Canal Company, so long as there shall be no permanent alteration in the level of low water of spring tides at Warrington Bridge, is to main- tain the same channel dredged which was originally dredged as aforesaid." And the court held there had been no such permanent alteration. IF it were necessary to deoide the question,Sir Robert Finlay gave most cogent reasons to show that there had been, in fact, a permanent alteration. Further, I am not pre- , pared to hold that the original obligation had ever been complied with. Mr. Justice Byrne gravely doubted it. But both these questions do not, in mv opinion, really arise. I am, with great respect, unable to agree in the view that the duty of the defendants is to be measured by any artificial standard at all. It is to be measured by the words that are found in the agreement scheduled to the Act of 1896. Suppose that low water of spring tides at Warrington Bridge is the same thing as the low water of which the Act speaks, and suppose that the original dredging was the same thing as the dredging required by the statute on the true construe- ' tion. Even so, I cannot see that, when a statutory agreement prescribes a particular standard, a court of law would be at liberty to substitute a standard, in itself different, on the ground that its effects are equivalent. Under the judgment of the Court of Appeal the Ship Canal Company would have to dredge less than under that of Mr. Justice Byrne, who construed the statutory agreement, and construed it rightly. I think that

THE ORDER OP THAT LEARNED JUDGE OUGHT TO BE

RESTORID on this point. What has been said applies to the other point on which the judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne was varied. The Court of Appeal declared that "the obligation of the Manchester Ship Canal Company to maintain the said channel dredged is not an obligation that the said channel shalt at all times be free o f obstruction, but is an oblightion to maintain the said channel dredged by removing obstructions as and when they arise within a reasonable time." Now, the statu- tory agreement says nothing about removing obligations bat it does say t4t the ship Canal Company shall " dredge " the channel, "and for ever after maintain the same dredged, so that at all times there shall be a depth of Bft. at low water of spring tides." The duty is not to dredge Bft. down from any datum line, real or imaginary, but to dredge so that there shall be Bft. at the specified times. It seems that the tide in this part of the river is apt to deposit great quantities of silt. It was sufficient, said Mr. Cripps for the defendants, that the channel should be dredged to a depth of Sft. below the low water level at Warrington Bridge, and when the silt came a reasonable time should be allowed to remove it. It seems to me that the best answer to this contention is to read without comment the language of the statu- tory agreement itself. The Ship Canal Company must either dredge deep enough to maintain the required depth of Bft., whatever be the amount of silt, or it must take steps forthwith to remove the silt. These obligations may be very onerous, but we have no right to alter them or to shrink from giving fall effect to them. There is

===== 0002601_19070601_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
PROMOTERS' CASE CLOSED.

The Select Committee on Thursday continued the consideration of the proposal of the Ship Canal Com- pany—embodied in their various powers Bill—to pump the estuarial water into the.upper reaches of the canal, with the object of raising its level. i Evidence n support of the scheme and against the contention of the opponents, the Corporation and traders of Warrington, that its effect would be to increase the saltness of the water which returns to the Mersey at Rixton, and is used by the manufacturers for feeding their boilers, was given by, Mr. F. Scudder, chemist to the Mersey and Irwell joint Committee; Sir Douglas Fox, C.E. ; Professor Frankland, Birming- ham University; and Mr. C. Escourt, city analyst of Manchester. The witnesses supported the opinion that the salt water pumped into the canal at Latchford would escape again with the opening of the locks, and in any case would not find its way up as far as Rixton. The promoter's case was closed, and Mr. Priest, engineer to the Corporation of Warrington, gave evid- enee as to the degree of salinity of the water in the various reaches of the canal at the present time, and submitted that the promoters were not justified in the view they had put forward of the effect of the proposed pumping. The Committee adjourned.

DREDGING THE RIVER.

A FAR-REACHING DECISION.

Victory of Warrington Traders.

Judgment was given in the House of Lords on Wednesday, in the appeal of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., and other traders and manufacturers at Warrington,against the Manchester Ship Canal Company. The appellants claimed the right to have a depth of Bft. of water at low water of spring tides in the channel between the western boundary of the works of Monks, Hall, and Co., at Bank Quay and Howley Lock, and, failing this, free navigation in respect of vessels pa,ssinh , through the Walton Lock portion of the canal, extend- ing from Eastham to Walton. The respondents failed, according to the appellants, to maintain a depth of Bft. at low water, and consequently the navigation of the river was obAructed. The respondents maintained that , they were only bound to maintain the channel dredged by removing obstructions as and when they should arise, and upon receipt of notice. The appellants, on the other hand, contended that the respondents had not performed their original obligation to dredge the channel to the depth and width required by the agreement of 196. Mr. Justice Byrne found in favour of the appellants, and that they were entitled to recover all tolls and dues paid since August, 1899, down to the date of the judgment. The Court of Appeal reversed this decision, and hence the appeal. They held that the obligation of the respondents to maintain the channel dredged was not an obligation that the channel should at all times be free of obstruc- tion, but was an obligation to maintain the channel dredged by removing obstructions as and when they arose within a reasonable time. • The Lord Chancellor: The controversy raised in this appeal depends, in my opinion, on the construction of a section in a private Act of Parliament. When the Manchester Ship Canal Company acquired its Parlia- mentary powers in 1885, it became laound to keep the river Mersey, throughout a specified section of its course, at a particular depth, for the benefit of the borough of Warrington and the traders thereof. Subse- quently, the statutory language imposing this duty was modified by an agreement scheduled to the Act of 1896, and the words now operative are as follows :—" The company shall forthwith, at their own cost, dredge the bed and banks of the river Mersey (meaning thereby a channel in and along the river Mersey having tt ruininitun width of fifty feet at the bottom), and for ever after maintain the same dredged I so that at all times there shall be a depth of eight feet

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETIC'S.

Cricket.

The West Lancashire League at present doos not offor much cause for comment as none of the 010b3 have ful- filled many fixtures. St. Helens at present lead and judging by the form which they have shown promise to be well in the running for championship honours at the close of the season. Warrington's position is somewhat lowly but as'they have only playe I two matches there is no occasion for apprehension and doabtiess the next few weeks will see them seriously challenging the leadership. In the second division Preston and Earles- town are bracketed equal with Warrington fourth in the list.

The league tables to date are :

Warrington first were without a fixture on Saturday and several of .their men turned out for the second eleven in their match with Walker's Brewery, and assisted very materially to secure the verdict. Batting first on a slow wicket the brewery men were unable to time the deliveries of Callon and Plinston and were all tumbled out for 32. Cook was the only player to do himself justice and was top scorer with 12. When they took the field Walker's played keenly but were unable to keep down the Warrington score which reached 120. Thewlis was the most prominent batsmen and his 38 was scored by vigorous and attractive play.

In the Warrington and District League St. James are a good second to Orford Barracks and in fact promise to wrest from them the honour of holding the first position, as with a match in hand they are only one point behind. The soldiers had an unusually exciting time on Saturday as can be judged from the fact that their game with Bewsey Wesleyans ended in a tie, both slabs scoring 87.

Out of four matches played Cheshire Lines Engineers have won one and on Saturday they were defeated by St. James. Batting first, Duckworth and Riley, by scoring 15 and 11 respectively enabled the score to be carried to 46. When St. James' came to bat none of their men did anything very brilliant, but there was some all round good play, and 54 was totalled before the last wicket fell.

Both Castle Rubber and Walker's Brewery have yet to win their first match as both have played three and lost three. On Saturday the first-named team met Mackie's eleven and went down. Batting first, the printers ran up 42 out of which Fell contributed 12. Woodhead and Burrell then worked havoc amongst the wickets with the result that Castle Rubber were all out for 25.

Batting on their own ground Grappenhall scored 56 agsinst Crosfleld's, Carter claiming 21 of the runs. Crosfield's opened their innings well, but later a rot set in and half of the men failed to score. Had Dixon, who was 14 not out, had a little more support there might have been a different tale to tell. As it was the soapmakers retired beaten by IS.

After several weeks of more or less indifferent work the Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones on Saturday found his form, and ran up 39 not out for Moore and Darqsbury against Penketh. Largely owing te his efforts his side won with ease, scoring 96 against 35. For Penketh, W. Stevens was the top scorer with 18.

Sankey Wire Mills had a light task set them on Satirday by Widnes White Rose, and ran out easy winners. For the losers J. Wilcock batted well for 16, whilst for the Wire Mills, Clarko (22) and Whitfield (10) were the principal scorers. The game between the Second Elevens requited also in a victory for the Sankey team,•anct to tuts end W. Garet was the principal con- tributor with 15, out of a total of 43.

Sankey and Arpley Athletic, two junior teams, met at Sankey on Saturday, and the victory rested with the homesters, who by all round play ran up 44. Out of 23 scored by their opponents Finch recorded 13, and was also the most successful batsman during the match.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

The next two weeks will witness the close of the first round of the Warrington Works Bowling competition, and then for smile weeks the team competitions will be suspended and the annual individual main opened. This is a new order of things and promises to lend additional interest to the close of the season as the finals in both the team and individual competition will take place on the same day. In the past the main has been no small undertaking and there is every indication that the num- ber of entries will this year be larger than ever. Persons desiring to take part in the competition should com- municate at once with the officers of the Association. The second round of the works tournament will consist of one match only—between aresnings and P. and K.— so as to prevent byes in future stages.

Last Saturday further progress was made in connec- tion with the first round, three matches being decided. The closest finish of the afternoon was between Thewßs and Griffiths and Castle Rubber, the first named team winning by eleven points. From the start the victors obtained the lead and though they suffered several r verses they never lost the initial advantage. The same thing applied to he games between Rylands and Monks, Hall and Crlsfields and Fletchers, the first named clubs in eaeh case leading throughout and winning with ease.

Some good trundling was seen during the course of the afternoon's play and in one game in particular between Deakin and flaslaro there was a long fight and twenty-five ends were played before Haslam claimed the verdict. After the opening chalks had been divided Haslam ran from 3-2 to 9-6 when Deakin again secured the block and ran to 12-11. Haslam equalized and then the players again divided the points until 14 all when Haslam got a good wood home. Going to the other extreme J. Dagnall, jun., only required ten ends in which to polish off his opponent and seven times he got both of his woods home.

Liberals and Conservatives indulged in a close fight over the latter's green in Sankey-street, the verdict being secured by the home team by 23 points. The Liberals were not altogether happy on the green, and it was some time before they could get going, as witness the faot that they lost the singles and won the doubles. It wai.; rather singular that W. Taylor and H. Fogg should both lose when playing singly, but when operat- ing together should turn the tables on J. Billing° and S. Caldwell by 13-1. To-day the Liberals meet the Saracen's Head players, and are hoping to taste the sweets of victory.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
Water Polo.

The Warrington Swimming 'Club opened their season last Tuesday, when they played the famous Hyde Seal team in a friendly match at Hyde. The result was a victory of 5 goals to 2 for the home team. This was not a great beating considering the difference in the teams. The first match in the Liverpool and District League Competition takes glace on Thursday evening next at the Baths, Legh-street, when Southport will be the visitors. This should prove to be a very good match and the locals should about win. The team will be selected on Tuesday evening.

The Committee commence the series of members' handicaps, which so successful last season, next Thurs-- day, when there will be a5O yards junior members' handicap. They have also reduced the prices on this occasion with the idea of making the interest in swim- ming and water polo more popular and it is to hoped that their efforts will be successful.

The various secretaries of the Works Athletic Clubs are reminded that the entrees for the Works Squadron Competition (Sir Gilbert Greenall Challenge Shield) close on Monday, June 10th, inclusive. Any particulars may be obtained from the Secretary of the Swimming Club.

The Committee are making qrrangements for the Liverpool Westminster Ladies' Swimming Club to come and give an exhibition of swimming and life saving. Further particulars will be announced later.

Catherine Owen, a widow, of Ty Hen, Llanfaelog, Anglesey, who has just died, aged ninety-three, lived for sixty-five years in the same hou3e, and during all those years only made one journey out of the county, and then only to Bangor, a few miles distant.

A CROSS APPEAL

on the ground that the plaintiffs, who are traders,cannot bring this action for damages and return of tolls paid by them under section 88 of the Act of 1883, and sections 1, 2. and 6 of the agreement of 1896, which was confirmed by the Act of 1896. It is said that the duty laid upon the Ship Canal Company by those Acts was a contractual duty, at all events, in part, and that as the plaintiffs were not parties to the contract they cannot sue for breach of it. I entirely agree with the view taken by the Court of Appeal affirming Mr. Justice Byrne on this part of the case, and have nothing further to say on the subject. I think the most convenient way of dealing with the case will be to restore the order of ltS Ongni..l variations inserted by the Court of Appeal, and I move your lordships in that sense. Lord James, Lord Robertson, and Lord Collins con- curred, and judgment was given Accordingly.

11 EIE EXAMINER.

JUNE Ist. 1907

We wish that the attitude THE of the Conservative party in GOVERNMENT the House of Commons in AND THE regard to our Colonial COLONIES. relationships were SS unintelligible as it looks. That it is unintelligent, reckless, frankly mis- chievous, there can be no doubt. It was curiously illustrated on Wednesday during question time. Mr. ARTHUR LEE wished to know whether there would be a provision in the Estimates for the expense of entertaining our Colonial visitors, and, if so, on which vote the question would arise ? Was it to be further understood, he further went on to inquire, that the Colonial Ministers were the guests, not of the Government, but of the nation? A supplementary question was evidently intended to be insulting. "May I ask" said Mr. LEE, speaking of Mr. WINSTON CHURCHL, ,6 whether he thinks that his Majesty's Government represent the country in this matter?" Queries of this kind do not embarass Mr. CHURCHILL. Drawing him is like drawing the badger. He answers with tooth and claw. The Government, of course, does not represent the nation. In such a case, party has nothing to do with the matter; but, if it were otherwise, the Opposition could have no right of complaint, for the Government has precisely followed the example set by Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. Mr. CHURCHILL,is'much more than equal to any of his antagonists. We do not solicit commissera- tion for him because he has to answer stupid or mischievous questions. He likes it. But we do protest most earnestly,and not without indignation, against the now long-continued effort of the Opposition to make trouble between ourselves and the Colonies. It began as soon as his Majesty's present Ministers accepted office, and it has gone on without intermission. On the occasion of the Natal rising, there was a deliberate attempt to incite the Natal Ministry to absolute defiance of Downing-street. Similar efforts were made in connection with the Transvaal. The few Con- servatives who sit on the Opposition benches of the House of Commons seam to have got it into their heads that it must be a good thing to pretend that they are in a special manner the friends of the Colonies. That is gross and palpable humbug, of course. The Colonial record of the Conservative party does not bear looking into. The historic utterance on the subject came from MT. DISRAELI, who wrote to the EARL OF CLAREN- DON that " the Colonies are a millstone round our necks." Mr. CHURCHILL on Wednesday expressed surprise that a member of the " constitu- tional " party should suggest that the Ministers I of the day, holding office under the Crown, havel not a right to speak on behalf of the nation. The

surprise was justified, but we shall take the liberty of believing that Mr. CHURCHILL has lost the capacity of being surprised by what may be said or suggested on the benches opposite. We have an Opposition which for the first time is unpatriotic in pursuit of a proposal which is merely a party cry. It does not hesitate to say or do what, if it produced any effect at all, could only tend towards the dis- ruption of the Empire. Fortunately, its efforts in that direction have failed as conspicuously as its efforts to carry the cause of Tariff Reform at the polls. The incessant speeches of Mr. DEAKIN and one of his colleagues from the Commonwealth of Australia are already forgotten. They failed to make the mischief they were intended to create. It is from General BOTHA that we hear what was the real feeling produced by the Imperial Conference among the sounder sort of Colonial Statesmen. General BOTHA., though he would not trust himself to speak our language, understood the mind of his Majesty'l3 Ministers. He knows that the present Govern- ment is as friendly to the Colonies as it is possible for any Government to be, and he also sees with great clearness that it is absurd to ask the Mother CoHntry, with all its teeming millions and all its burden of taxation, to change its fiscal system on behalf of Colonies whose average of prosperity is much higher than its own.

There is only a light COUNCILLOR STOTT '8 agende for next Tuesday's LITTLE PROGRAMME. Council meeting, and it would be still lighter but for the programme of special business which Coun- cillor Stott has introduced with a zeal for the public welfare which can be commended. No less than three topics claim Councillor Stott's atten- tion, and each one is of considerable interest. First of all he will introduce a motion to obtain a return of the expenses paid to each member of the Council for serving on deputations during the three years ending May 31st, 1007. Whether or no the Labour member suspects that there hare been uncalled-for junketings at the ratepayers' expense we do not know, but the information he seeks may be instructive. On the minutes of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee coming up Councillor Stott will interrogate the Chairman with reference to the provision of additional and cheaper bathing facilities, a resolution in favour of which was passed last December. Apparently nothing has been done, and Councillor Stott wishes to know why, and when the Committee proposes to take up the scheme. Another batch of questions he has in pickle for the Chairman of the General Purposes Committee with regard to the often shelved widening of Warrington Bridge, and the construction of a new bridge at Howley. The Chairman of this committee is the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) who used to be more i pertinacious than anyone n regard to this self-same scheme. We cannot believe that he has lost all interest in it since he ascended the Mayoral chair and here, at any rate, the questioner should be sure of a sympathetic reply. It would make Alderman Smethurst's mayoralty memorable if he could carry through these great improvements during his term of office. He has now an unequalled opportunity, and we hope he will take advantage of it.

The protracted litigation WARRINGTON AND between the traders of THE SHIP CANAL. Warrington and the Man- chester Ship Canal Company with reference to the dredging of the Mersey has ended in a great victory for the traders. The House of Lords on Wednesday delivered judgment in the appeal of the traders against the findings of the Court of Appeal, and the result is that the decision of the late Mr. Justice Byrne is upheld and the company are bound to fulfil their statu- tory obligations to preserve a channel of a depth of Bft. at low water of spring tides. The decision is a momentous one and its effects will be far reach- ing. The obligations enforced upon the company are onerous but in order to facilitate the passing- of the Ship Canal Bill the promoters certainly agreed to maintain a dredged channel in the Mersey at Warrington and they should not be allowed to evade their responsibilities. It is of immense importance to the welfare of the town that the river traffic should not be impeded and when it is stated that one firm alone carries something like 12,000 tons per month on the Mersey to and from Warrington it will be realized with what great issues this dispute has been fraught. The carrying trade of Warrington with Liverpool is, we believe, many thousands of tons per month, and it is a great advan- tage to the traders to take their goods down the river to Liverpool and ship direct there, alongside the great iliners. The construction of the canal has made it necessary that the solid material which is brought up by the tides and deposited in the reach of the river at Wurington must be removed by dredger instead of as formerly by water. The remit of the Lords' findings is that Wa rrington's carrying trade is permanently secured. Owing to the state of the river, however, the traders have been obliged to make frequent use of the canal and a toll has been levied. Under the judgment, this money has to be returned. The traders are to be congratulated upon their victory, and the only pity is that the matter should have aroused 80 much antagonism and litigation with the company. Meanwhile the new Ship Canal Bill is under the consideration of a Com- mittee of the House of Commons and the War- rington Corporation and traders are offering strong opposition on the ground that the proposals would involve considerable loss to the traders of the town owing to the increased salinity of the water which it is feared would result. This question is also a serious one for Warrington, and we hope the Committee will see that the interests of the town do not suffer.

The agate, a silicious stone THE GEM OF used in jewellery, a variety JUNE. of chalcedony, is symbolical of June. Its meaning is " Health and Long Life," and in the zodiacal signs the agate denotes Scorpio. In ancient folk-lore, the agate was supposed to render a person invisible and to turn swords of foes against themselves. But when Shakespeare men tions,in "Romeo and Juliet," "In shape no bigger than an agate-stone," he does not refer to the gem, but to a fount in printing called the "agate," which was smaller than our pre- sent "minion." The lapidary, or artist who engraves precious stories, thinks highly of the agate with its bands of various colours blended together, as it can be cut in an exquisite manner. So, in "Mach Ado," we have a reproach to the engraver in the line, "An Agate very vilely cut." Again in "Love's Labour's Lost," an inhuman brute is described as having a "heart like an agate." But, in "Henry 1V.," the words," I was never manned with an agate till now," have a diflerent significance. No master of language ever played more wonderfully with the meanings of words than did the Bard of Avon.' The term agate formerly meant a very diminutive person, Queen Mab being no bigger than an agate- stone on the fore-finger of an alderman, so that an ordinary man compared as a colossal giant beside her. The origin of the name was due to the river Achates in Sicilly, where sapphires, pearls, ernerals, and ruby-red " achates" were found in abundance.

For a second Royal Spanish ANOTHER SPANISH wedding the bride comes WEDDING. from what may now be called an English family. The recent Royal birth displaced a little heir- presumptive; it is his widowed father who is to he the bridegroom. The bride comes from a Twickenham family. She it, the Princess Louise of Orleans, xv hose three elder sisters (one of them the Queen of Portugal, was boro by the River Thames. Her brother, of course, is the Duo d'Orleans, the Royalist Pretender to the French throne, and it was a curious coin- cidence that when, a few months ago, he was in England for the Requiem Mass at Wey-

.01.1.74,111'

bridge for the repose of the Comte de Paris's 9„eol, the Bonapartist Pretender, Prince Victor 24apoleon, was also in London. The Comtesse de Paps has always been on good terms with the British Royal Family. The Duke, like all Pre tenders who know their business, is still looking for the happy day when his throne shall be res- tored to him. "when the day comes I shall know how to reward," he wrote, in the true Royal fashion, to a Deputy of the French Chamber about a twelvemonth ago. The other Pretender, in a lianifesto, has declared that if circumstances " called upon me to place my life and death at the service of France, I should come" Princess Louise has a younger brother in the Spanish navy. Don 9arlos, whom she weds, is King Alfonso's brother- -11?-law, his wife being the Princess of Asturias who died three years ago. Of Bourbon-Sicily, he was duly naturalised in his late wife's country. He is expected to fetch his bride from England, where the will be married from Wood Norton, the resid- Epee of her remarkable brother and head of the 'ersily, the Due d'Orleans.

I have spent Sabbaths in most of the cities of the Continent of Europe, but I have never found any as bad as Chicago. I admit at once that most of the cities of the States are better than Chicago —but the the observance or non-observance of the Sabbath in the States is going to be one of the great tests of the best life of the great Republic.—ltev. Town Council.

The Horse Parade.

The monthly meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tuesday evening in the Town Hall at 6 o'clock.

A collection was taken en route and realised £7 9s. 5d., of vrhieh £5 has been handed over to the Infirmary and £2 9s. sd. to the District Nursing Association. A description of the parade appears on page O.

The Price of Bread. As far as Warrington is concerned the members of the Master Baker's Association met on Tuesday even- ing last to consider the price of bread. There is a great feeling all round that the bakers would be justified in raising the price of bread a d. per 41b. loaf, but through the unsatisfactory state of the bread trade in Warrington at the present time, it was thought advisable to adjourn the meeting for a fort- night to see whether the advance would be maintained. The advance up to now is ss. 6d. to Gs. per sack. In the meactime we are asked to remind the public that it would be greatly to their interest to insist on having their bread weighed, and the weight distinctly stated at the time of purchase.

WARRINGTON

Coming Events. From our advertising columns it will be seen ihat a special public service is to be held at Golborne-street Baptist church on Thursday when Rev. J. Yonge will preach at 7.30. At this service a presentation will be made to the church of a handsome carved oak communion table, given by the Independent Order of Rechabites as a tribute to the memory of the late Mr. Thomas Hall, who had for a long period been actively connected with the society in many of its various offices. There will at the same time be presented to the church and school, a platform, desk and chair, an enlarged framed photograph and a set of carved oak collecting plates, these being the gifts of members and friends who desire to place on record their esteem of the late Mr. Hall, in respect to his long and honoured connection with the church in the formation of which Mr. Hall was the prime mover. The meeting will be public and a special invitation is extended to all Rechabites. The articles enumerated are now on view at Messrs. J. M. Greenhalgh and Co's., Bridge- street.
