Fatal Fall. Mr. S. Brighouse,county coroner, held an inquest at Warrington last evening on the body of Julia Cunningham (37), wife of Martin Cunningham, 5, West View, Orfard, who died in the Workhouse Hospital on Thursday. It appears that the deceased for some time had suffered from heart troubles and frequently had to get up during the night. On the morning of May 19th, she was found at the bottom of the stairs by her daughter. She had apparently fallen from the top and was much bruised about the body. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

Above all Wear BANK3'S HATS and CAPS .—Advt. School Attendance Officer's Warm Reception. At the Borough Police Court on Mthaday Catherine Bent, George-street, was summoned for assaulting William Holbrook, a school attendance officer. Mr. A. T. Hallaway (deputy town-clerk) prosecuted, and said that on the 13th of May the officer was executing a summons on the defendant for her son's non- attendance at school, when she treated him in a most disgraceful manner, threatening "to stick him," and using very abusive language. The assault com- plained of was only of a trivial character, the defend- ant striking him with a piece of paper, which she rolled into a ball and threw at him. Defendant, who expressed her sorrow foq he action, was fined 10s.

Honour for a Volunteer Officer. We are pleased to announce that the Volunteer offiryno. nocuration has been conferred upon Captain ' and Quartermaster Arthur Flaxman Godfrey Potter, of the let Volunteer Battalion (P.W.V.) South Lanca- shire Regiment. The distinction has certainly been well earned, for Captain Potter has a splendid record of service to his credit, and is one of the most highly respected members of the Battalion. He joined the Volunteers on August 6th, 1873, and having served in the ranks for twenty-six years was appointed Quartermaster on November Bth, 1899, with the rank of lieutenant. On May 12th 1903, he was further promoted to an honorary captaincy, retaining the office of Quartermaster. He has worked indefatig- ably in his special department and the members of the Battalion together with his many friends among the townspeople generally will be gratified to hear of this recognition of him long moririone. Captain Potter is at present encamped with the Duke of Lancaster's Yeomanry at Aston-on-Trent.

• NORFOLK SUITS from 18/6—BANKs,Outfltter.—Advt. Foreman Platelayer's Sudden Death. An inquest was held on Saturday at Warrington on the body of Samuel Plumb, a foreman platelayer, in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company, who lived at 318,Liverpool-road, Sankey. It appeared that on Wednesday morning, after going to work,Plumb returned home for breakfast,when he com- plained of a pain in his side and died almost imme- diately. Dr. Smith,who made a post-mortem examina- tion, attributed death to failure of the heart's action, and a verdict of " Death from natural causes "was returned. —The funeral took place on Sunday after- noon at Sankey Cemetery, the service being conducted by the Rev. J. R. Jones. The chief mourners were : —Mrs. Plumb (widow), Mr. Fred Plumb, Mr. Samuel Plumb, gr. Tom Plumb (sons), Miss Lily Plumb, Miss Nellie Plumb, Miss Polly Plumb (daughters), Mr. Joseph Plumb and Mr. John Plumb, Little Leigh (brothers). Preceding the hearse were the following members of the railway company's staff H. Minshall (permanent way inspector), Inspectors Lloyd, and Holbrook-, Mr. Sam Faulkner (ganger), Mr. Vernon (signalman), Mr. Morley (brakesman), with Messrs. Hindley, Shaw, Fairclough, Foddy, Adams, Thomas, Clare, Armitage, Foster, Carter, Edleston, Dutton Cliffe, Reaves, and Walton. The bearers were : Messrs. T. Bennett, Thomas Chester, J. Davidson, Fred Massey, W. Lawton, W. J. Holden, and J. Antrobus. Floral tributes were sent by The widow and family, Mr. Joseph Plumb (Little Leigh), Mr. and Mrs. John Plumb (Little Leigh), Mrs. Gannon, Annie, Polly and Nellie ,Plumb (Little Leigh), Mrs. Dolan, Mr. Llewellyn (Sloop Hotel), Mr. Roughley (Black Horse), Mr. Walter Cooper (Widnes), Mr. Grimshaw (Gate Wharf Farm), " Fellow workmen," " Neigh- bours at Sankey Bridges," and the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants (Warrington branch).

ANOTHER COP FOR WARRINGtON.

Crosfield's Band's Victory at New Brighton.

Messrs. Crosfield's brass band achieved an excep- tionally fine victory on Saturday, whea they carried off the first prize in the ninth annual contest held at New Brighton Tower. Fifteen bands competed— Horwich Old, Rushden Temperance, Widnes St. Mary's, Litherland Silver, Shaw, Pemberton Old, Wyke, Gossage's, Mountain Ash, Parr Temperance, Lindley, Crosfield's, Mara St. John's, Goodshaw, Irwell Springs. Two others —lrwell Bank, Bolton and Guide Temperance, Darsven—had entered but failed to put in an appearance. The adjudicator was Jr. Fenton Renshaw, Huddersfield, and he awarded the first prize of .f.,30 in cash, a silver- mounted baton in case, and the Tower Challenge Cup value 50 guineas to Crosfield's band which was under the conductorship of Mr. W. Halliwell. The test piece was "Ii Trovatore " and in his remarks on Messrs. Crosfield's rendering (as reported in Wright and Round's " Brass Band News ") the adjudicator said :—" Allegro—a good opening ; a good toned band; p. very nice; soprano excellent; if. a good massive tone. Allegro—accompaniments nicely sub- dued and well kept under ; trombone playing very nicely; meno masso-fairly handled, all expression marks well observed; duet cadenza fairly rendered. Vivace very good. Andantino— accompaniments very well kept under; solo cornet playing with great taste; soprano very good; move- ment very artistically rendered. Allegro—capitally rendered in all respects; euphonium cadenza excellent. Largo—accompaniments nicely subdued and quite a feature; euphonium an artiste playing with great taste ; cadenza excellent. Allegro moderato maestoso —This movement was well handled, the only fault I had to find being that one of the solo cornets was a ••”-seo- Andante (" Chorus excellent. Andante (a-o)—Ac...uwpautannts a reature here; horn playing with great taste; when soprano joins it was excellent, in such nice sympathy; soprano cadenza was excellent. Allegro—excellently given ; the bass trombone built up well in the f. movements, and is evidently a good player. Poco pin mosso—a capital finish to a good performance, and best so far. Other bands were placed as follows :-2,lrwell Springs; 3, Lindley Prize; 4, Parr Temperance; 5, Horwich Old; 6, Shaw; 7, Goodshaw. The band had a hearty reception on returning to Warrington on Saturday evening, a crowd assembling and raising hearty cheers. Selections were played en ,out to the works where the victorious instru- mentalists were met by the Works Manager, Mr. F. J. Lord and warmly congratulated. [ The cup was sent to Warrington on Thursday evening by the T. 30 train from Liverpool and was met at the 'Central Station by the band and members of the eom- mittee. A procession was formed and traversed the principal streets, the trophy being exhibited from a landau. Large crowds lined the route and it was evident that the success of the band has given great [ satisfaction to the townspeople. On arrival at the works the cup was filled and success to the band was ' heartily drunk. Afterwards the band marched round the Bank Quay district playinc, selections. Mr. A. H. Crosfield M. 11., is to entertain the band in the course of a few days. The band has entered for several other notable contests including Belle Vue, Newtown (Wales) and Hereford.

P. E. GAFFIKIN, Acting Medical Officer of Health.

Once again the Watch Committee has moved in the matter of obstructions in the streets caused by goods and other articles overhanging footpaths. The obstructions show no signs of moving, however, though the police have been instructed to enforce the law. We have many times called attention to the carcases of meet, the strings of boots, and the too low window blinds which seriously incommode the users of ou r street footpaths. Many of these things are abomina- tions which ought to be entirely abolished, and we hope the police will do their duty. There is no harm to tradesmen involved.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 1, 1907

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES ARSON PRISONER RELEASED.

John Joseph Marsh, who was convicted at the Liver- pool November Assizes for arson at Widnes, and sentenced to twelve months' imprisonment which was subsequently reduced to a term of six months, %vas released from Walton Gaol on Saturday morning. By arrangement he was liberated after the rest of the prisoners had gone. Marsh said he had been treated with the greatest kindness by the officials and warders. For over four months he had been con- fined in the prison hospital as the result of influenza which left a serious affection of the throat. Referring to his conviction,he emphatically protested his innocence of the charge, and it is reported that something will shortly be done to secure a Government inquiry on the lines of those followed in the Edalji cage. ST. PAUL'S CHURCH, WIDNES.

On Sunday special services in celebration of the anniversary of the above church were hold. The preacher at the morning, afternoonand evening service, was the Rev. G. H. St. P. Garrett, vicar of Horwich, and formerly vicar of Widnes. The morning service was well attended. In the afternoon a toy and flower service were held at which Mr. Garnett preached. The scholars of the Sunday school met at the Parochial Hall and, about thirteen hundred strong, marched in pro- cession to the church. They were headed by the school banner, and as many of the children carried bunches of flower the effect was very pretty. During the service the children walked to the communion table where their gifts of flowers and toys were received by the Rev. D. E. Morton (vicar) and the Rev. N. J. Poole. These will be forwarded to the hospitals and the homes of the sick and poor. In the evening the church was crowded to the utmost capacity when the Rev. C. H. St. P. Jarrett preached an eloquent sermon. The singing of the choir, which was augmentesl by a number of girls from the Sunday school, was very pleasing. The offertories, which are on behalf of the Sunday school funds, amounted £23. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The fortnightly Imeeting of the Runcorn Rural District Covncil was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present :—Nlessrs. G. Pilling, T. Baxter, J. Spruce, C. E. Linaker' T. Wright, S. Woodward, P. Booth, A, Haining, J. Renshaw E. Britch, J. INlynekyme Antwis, 1 t •3. 13:11, W. Tamer, W. Weir, H. Mawdsley, J. B. Chadwick, J. H. Davies, G. Egerton Warburton, Admiral Jones Parry,j. L. Jones' A. Broome, J. Hallows, E. Bretherton, W. Winstanlay, J. Bolshaw, C. Horton, J. Whitlow, NV. Harrison G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors), J. Farrington (inspector), and Dr. Adams (medical officer). The clerk intimated that he had received Con missi .of Woods and Forests stating the ruey were. tion to issue the posters asked by the District Council in respect to small holdings on Crown Lands. Mr. Linaker moved that the members of the Council who knew of persons desiring small holdings should inform the Clerk. The Government professed to believe that there was a demand for small holdings but he did not and was convinced that the proposal woold end in smoke. The Government were seeking to establish the extent to which the desire for small hold- ings existed. Mr. Linaker's motion was approved. The Blaydon Urban Council asked the Council to support the following memorial to the Local Govern- ment Board: "That your memorialists desire to chll your attention to the great hardships suffered by the ratepayers in general in urban and rural districts owing to their being called upon from time to time to provide long lengths of sewers at great expense for the purpose of taking the sewage away from isolated small groups of houses built in places at considerable distances from main sewers or sewer outfalls, and that persons respon- sible for the erection of such houses should pay two thirds or at any rate a half of the cost." Mr. Antwis moved that the resolution be supported. Mr. Linaker said he did not think it quite fair that ratepayers should have to pay any proportion of the cost of making the sewer's at all. He thought the charge which represented capital should be a charge upon the land and that the maintenance should be a charge upon the occupiers. The matter was rererred to the Parliamentary Committee. The Clerk read a communication from the County Council estimating their willingness to treat certain main roads in the district with calcium chloride or dust laying material providing the District Council or other persons would contribute two thirds of the I cost. The expense would be from £l5 to £4O a mile. Mr. Antwis : I move nothing be done. Mr. Woodward thought that the Parish Councils in tke should be allowed to take the question up if desired. Mr. Chadwick moved that the matter be referred to the Hghways Executive Committee, and this was approved. The special Committee appointed to consider the proposals of the Local Government Board that the management of the Public Health and Housing Act should be taken from the hands of the District Councils and transferred to the City Council, recommended that the Council protest against the proposal. Mr. Linaker said that the proposal would not mean better administration and would certainly, if carried out cost more. The Clerk and Mr. Linaker were appointed delegetes to the Rural District Councils Association Conference, in June to consider the question.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS CYCLING FATALITY. On Monday evening Thomas Foster, aged sixteen, son of a well-known St. Helens grocer, died under sad cir- cumstances. Borrowing a bicycle belonging to one of his father's employe's he went for a ride. While pro- ceeding up Westileld-street he was observed to relax hold of the handles, and his head appeared to strike the kerbstones. He was carried into a neighbouring house and Dr.Lathom was sent fot,but the doctor could find no trace of injury. The youth mas a promising agricultural student, having recently won a ten pounds prize at Preston.

ST. HELENS SUNDAY GAMBLING. On Monday ii the St. Helens Police 03urt, nine young men were summoned on a charge of gaming with cards,and thirteen young men wen, charged with gaining. Th 4 offences took place on Sunday afternoon last; and in imposing a fine of 108, and costs the chairman said the magistrates were determined, if possible, to put a stop to this Sunday afternoon gaming and gaming of any sort. LEGS AND ARMS GOING LIKE A WINDMILL. On Monday, in St. Helens, a labourer named Martin Brannon, of no fixed address, was brought upon a charge of begging, being drunk and disorderly, and assaulting Constable Rignall. The Constable stated that on Saturday afternoon he was in Baldwin-street, when he saw the defendant, who went to some ladies who had bunches of bluebells in their hands and asked for some. They took no notice of him, and he then asked them to give him a couple of coppers. As he was very drunk, witness told him to pull himself together and go home whereupon he commenced using disgusting language. He WM than taken into custody, when he commenced striking and kicking him, assistance having to be obtained to take him to the police station. Another Constable stated that prisoner's legs and arms were going like a windmill.—TheChief Constable stated that prisoner had been before the court eighty times previ- ously. He tore his trousers into shreds on Saturday night, and they had to find him a fresh pair.— Prisoner was sent to prison for seven days for begging, twenty-eight days for being drunk and disorderly, and a further twenty-eight days for the assault.

DEATH OF MRS. WOOD.

General regret was occasioned throughout the district by the death of Mrs. Wood, wife of Mr. F'. J. Wood, ' who passed away at the Willows, Newton, on Sunday. IThe funeral on Tuesday was the occasion of a striking , demonstration of respect. The first part of the service was held in Newton Church, the opening voluntary being " 0 rest in the Lord." The choir then sang the hymns, "I heard the voice of Jesus say," and " Hark, hark, my , soul," the concluding voluntary being Dead March in Saul." The mourners then left the church, preceded by the organist and choristers, who walked to the Cemetery in their surplices. The carriages conveying the mourners contained Mr. F. B. Wood (the husbind), Mr. Arden Wood (son), Colonel Wood (brother-in-law), Mr. W. Clarke. Lowton; Mr. G. D. B. Smith, Dr. Watkins, Mr. Robert Stone, Mr. Borron, Mr. Alex. Vicars, Mr. and Mrs. Norman McCorquodale, Mr. and Mrs. A. C. Ryder officiated assi.ded by Rev. H. Bass. At the graveside the choris- ters sang the hymn "Jean, Lover of my soul," led by the organist and choirmaster, Mr. Leyland. All the arrangements for the funeral were carried out by Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, Warrington, under the abie superintendence of Mr. Nicholson.

MR. J. A. SEDDON M.P., AT LIVERPOOL.

Speaking at the Conference of the National Union of Dock Labourers at Liverpool on Monday, Mr. J. A. Seddon, said he was not one of those who looked on the docker as an unskilled worker. Anyone who witnessed work at the docks must be impressed with the skill and agility which were required. The great evil of the docker's employment arose out of the fact that in the past it had suffered from great incursions of non- unionism. Hitherto the docks had been the dumping- ground for unemployed men of all kinds. The agricul- tural labourer, when tired of the country, came to the city, in the hope of finding an El Dorado at the docks, and owing to the lack of organisation it had been within the power of the employers to use every class of the large supplies of labour which presented itself, to the detriment of the regular skilled dockers. There was a feeling now amongst workers of all kinds that they were not enjoying their fall share of the wealth they were producing, and the time had arrived when guerilla warfare was not effective enough He was looking forward to the time when all branches of the distributive and traasport trades—clerks, shop assistants, carters, and dockers—would be federated, so that in the event of one section suffering injustice all the others would come to its help. When they went a step further, and combined the distributive trades with the producers, trade unionism would have entered into its full possession. However powerful trade unionism might become, labour would never get its rights unless it was represented in Parliament. Without Parlia- mentary action their movement would be like rowing a one-oared boat—always moving, but never going for- ward. In the past twelve months labour had gained more in legislation than in any previous ten years of Parliament. Mr. Seddon was also present at a luncheon given by the Liverpool Chamber of Commerce on Monday. In responding to the toast of" The Houses of Parliament" he said it was a distinct honour to him, as a oountry member and one belonging to the Labour party, which was not very numerously represented at that table —(a laugh) —to be associated with the toast. He had come to these meetings that day partly out of curiosity. (Laughter.), During the past year the pertinacity of the secretary of the Liverpool Chamber of Commerce had seemed to get upon his nerves. (Renewed laughter.) Lest year thequt.ion ra arose in the douse of Commons, and he (Mr. Seddon) held a distinct opinion, based on very slender founda- tions—not an uncommon basis for many political opinions—(laughter)—that the position of the status quo ante was correct. Mr. Barker, however, gave him much information, and brought him to the conclusion that a case had been made out for an amendment of ttle law—(hear, nemo—aid o by voice and vote he sup- ported the abolition of the light dues. (Applause.) As far as this country was concerned, there was a danger that each class would get into a separate and watertight compartment and thus lose a sense of the community of interest which spelled progress for the country. As R representative of Labour,he felt an interest in commerce equally with the members of that Chamber. (Hear, hear). The Liverpool Chamber of Commerce was doing a good educational work through its able President, Council, and Secretary, and he wished it long life and vigour in the extension of the commerce and of the prosperity of the Liverpool people. (Applause). EARLESTOWN

Mr. B. J. Greenwood, Chairman of the Vulcap Foundry, Ltd., visited the new premises in Bridge-street on Thursday last, the occasion having been looked forward to with great interest. Tea was provided, the catering and arrangements being all carried out by the committee and helpers. For the meeting which followed, the lecture hall, which accommodates 160, was crowded with young men. Mr. Profit (chairman of Committee) presided, supported by Messrs. B. J. Greenwood, W. Collingwood. W. Ross (Lancashire's Travelling Secre- tary), B. Corlett, Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Drs. Mouncey and Latham, 11. Pearson (hon. sec.), W. S. Dring (St. Helens), and others. Apologies for, absence were read from Col. Pilk.ington, C. 8., Rev. S. C. Hall, Dr. Valentine, Mr. G. Dring and Mr. T. Molyneux (who was seriously —Mr. Platt sang" The old brigade," Mr. G. Edmonds executed a violin solo, "Ii Trovatore," and Mr. J. Atkinson recited" A life for a life," each item being well given.—Mr. Profit, in a neat address, expressed the hope that the change from Fairclough-street to Bridge- street would be symbolic and prophetic of much in the association, and that the principles upon which they had hitherto worked would be in all their hearts, the prin- ciples of truth, righteousness, and love. Christ would not be content to be merely an outer circle of their lives. If he was to have any place at all it must be in the very centre of their lives. —Mr. Greenwood said he was very glad to attend that meeting, because he believed in Y.M.C.A. work wherever it was found. He was glad they had such a hearty and flourishing Association. There were many advantages in Y.M.C.A. work, one of whinh was that it enabled a young man to decide on which side he wouldstand, and to some extant it enabled him to make a confession of the resolution he had arrived at. Most of those present would be known in the town as Y.M.C.A. men. Some would say "Oh, he's a saint— one of that Y.M. Christian lot !" Well when they heard that sort of thing for the first time, especially young men, they felt like blushing, a sort of feeling of of being ashamed. Why that was so he had never yet been able to discover. They were not ashamed of being Brigltgrmen, thereonkl-tmcl ;.. th,,iwt.''- Why then should a man be ashamed of being a Christian? If a man was living an unclean life he had got something to be ashameMof ; if a life of falsehood and treachery he had something to be ashamed of ; but a man living an honest Christian life had nothing to be ashamed of. If they looked back on those who had preceded them they found the men who had followed Christ had been the brightest and most glorious men who ever trod God's earth. Nearly all the institutions for the suffering and afflicted and the great beneficent works, if traced back to their origin would be found to have been started and sup- ported by Christian men. if they sometimes had a little feeling of shame they should be ashamed of them- selves that they were ashamed. The Association also helped them to make it known on what side they were. To be a secret disciple was to labour under a very great disadvantage, coming out enabled them to bear witness in a way they otherwise could not.— Mr. Collingwood said he would like to say how very much he had appreciated meeting them and to see so many familiar faces. It was a 'great surprise to him to see the wide strides the Association had made. He had no idea of seeing such a large gathering of young men—he might say the life of the township. That Association should be the means of spreading the blessed truths xvhiph the Association held so very dear. Some people did not want to be thought saintly, and were ashamed to let their light shine before men, but it was a solemn duty for all Christians. He knew from his own experience how comradeship helped 11 man to carry out that duty, the greatest ' duty in life, and to honour God. (Applause,) I A vote of thanks to Mr. Greenwood and Mr. Coiling- ' wood was moved by Mr. Pearson, seconded by Mr. I Jackson, and eloquently supported by Mr. W. Ross, the I latter's points being that the Y.M.C.A. was one of the greatest brotherhoods in the world; one of the best missionary agencies, especially in the Eastern countries, and was now carrying on a movement in the Volunteer Camps which no other organisation touched. Mr. Pearson recited" What is a gentleman ?" and "Devil may care," after which Mr. Greenwood replied. After a sacred song by Mr. Platt, the meeting closed with the Doxology and Benediction. Mr. W. Pearson played the pianoforte accompaniments with skill and ability.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to Jocial workers by contributing the Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. • M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Exanariner " 05ce, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

Mr. Charles E. Parker has been good enough to express his opinion concerning the Labour Party in a letter which appeared in "The. Rxaminer " of Saturday last. He said : "Of political amciations the Labour party is the one which stands most for peace amongst nations. This does not at all arise because those who compose it are in any way deficient in the fighting instinct. They have their full share of the original savage. Nor does it arise from the fact that they are less patriotic than other classes. It springs from a recognition of other interests which appeal to them quite as powerfully as that form of patriotism, the final assertion of which may require the use of the sword. The unionist miners of England are far more in sympathy with their fellow workers in the same industry in Belgium, France, and America than they are with the wealthy capitalists who control the English mines. The Socialists of England have more in sympathy with the Socialists of Germany than they have with those in their own land who neither toil nor spin." Will every peace-loving citizen, every honest reformer, every Sunday school teacher, every intelli- gent seeker after righteousness make a note of the fact that" of political associations the Labour Party is the one which stands for peace am ingst nations." The Brotherhood of Man is something more to Socialists than mere sentimental pilll3,piously uttered, and quickly forgotten. They admit that they have their" full share of the original savage." They would not forget it. They are striving to overcome it, though; and that is something which their friends in the other political associations will, I hope, sooner or I later, endeavour to emulate. "Peace on earth good- will towards men" may then be an accomplished fact. Will all the Liberals of Warrington and district please to remember that the Labour Party is the pioneer in the peace movement.? Socialists or, at least, some of them, may not go to church as regularly as some folks would like but Jesus Christ is a living factor in their movement for righteousness, nevertheless. They are striving after a new state of society in which; ignorance, poverty, 'grime, and misery shall be unknown. Personally, I cannot understand why some "clever" individuals will look with reproach upon a Socialist. Of course, I do not forget that the terms Christian, Liberal, Tory were not names of endearment. I aria pot complaining. lam simply lost in wonderment at their littleness, at their want of perspicacity. Their faculty of observation is not yet developed, and the sooner they get some food for the brain the mere quickly they will grasp the fact that it is more easy to be selfish ttian unselfish, to be cruel than kind, to be -- ignorant than learned, to be a Liberal or Tore than a The Brabazon Society is doing a noble work in, Socialist. If they would only think sometimes, there brightening the lives of inmates of the Workhouse . would be some hope for them! A few more evening hospital by providing them with pleasant and profitable drives in the country, and a few more trips to the I employment. Nor does this exhaust its usefulness, for .• the Society is also the means of giving much-needed seaside, and then, perhaps, they may acquire a wider help to people on leaving the hospital, while some are• outlook ! sent to the seaside to recuperate. INcoa.--It is characteristic of his fairness. He will join the ranks of Socialism before long, or I am mis- taken. Thanks for "cutting." H.P.G. and ALPILA,.—I would advise you to read " Real Socialism : What it is and what it is not," by Robert BLatchford. The price is one penny. On page 7 you will find: "Another charge against Socialists is that they are Atheists, whose aim is to destroy all religion, and all morality. This is not true. It is true that many Socialists are Agnostics, and some are Atheists. But Atheism is no more a' part of Socialism than it is a part of Toryism, or of Radicalism or of Liberalism. Many prominent Socialists are Christians, not a few are clergymen. Many Liberal and Tory leaders are Agnostics or Atheists. I, for instance, am an Agnostic ; but so, I believe, are Mr. Balfour and Mr. John Morley. Mr. Bradlahgh was a Radical, and an Atheist; Professor Huxley was an opponent of Socialism, and an Agnostic. It deals with laws, and with industrial and political government. It is not sense to say, because some Atheists are Socialists, that all Socialists are Atheists.

HOW THE POOR LIVE.

"I am the mother of twenty-two children, drive four looms all the week, go out cleaning on Sunday, and do all my own washing and cleaning," said Hannah Briailey, who summoned her husband at Preston on Wednesday for persistent ctuelty. She had had five black eyes, she said., in nine weeks, and hundreds besides. Her husband loafed alynit the market-place. Foal- children were alive, and she had never lost an hour at the mill for elt‘ven years. A separation order with Gs. a week FM granted." Who ought to receive an old-age pension in this case—the hard-working mother of:twenty-two children or the lazy loafer of a husband and fattier? The woman performs the duty to the State and the !VIII is efrag-tipmr-fiociety ingneral and-IN-a 'vrOna an in particular. Doea Society need re-organising, think you?

TOTES AND GAS STOVES.

Sir,—The Trades Council of Warrington has taken a decisive step recently in deciding to contest elections with direct trade union representatives. Its action in this matter is open to some objection and a great deal will depend on the calibre of its champions at election times. At least, however, one aspect of the question has received emphasis, since it has again been declared that trades unionism of itself is insufficient to secure the workers their full rights. Political action is necessary to supplement industrial combinations. Trade unionism has been the stepping stone to direct representation, but if this continues to b 3 the natural order of events, the unionists of Warrington need to consolidate their influence locally and bring in many workers outside the pale. Men who have not suffi- cient industrial sense to join the union in their par- ticular trade are hardly likely to use politics intelli- gently. If the present intention of the Trades Council is to be of permanent value the special objects and propaganda of Trades Unionism must be more actively and persistently brought to the notice of the workers. I Under the present industrial conditions neither political action nor trades combination will of itself "fill the bill!' Both sides of the workers' case must be' presented to him and others and the union that ensures a large trade membership is the one that will, in times of inevitable industrial stress, be able to also continue its political warfare. Political action may secure the workers a larger share of the loaf in due time, but the crust necessary for "Little Mary" is ensured by benefits obtained through the Trade Union. The work waiting to be done is heavy, as may be llustrated by the gas stove workers in Warrington.

KiEr!llWal

Two of .the three local firms have world wide reputa- tions and employ a large number of men. It is esti- mated that at least 300 of these men are eligible for membership in either the National Union of Stove Grate Workers or in the Scotch Union ; yet the num- ber of local members is a paltry thirty or so. The- conditions of employment under the two firms are not very bad as things go, but one wonders what would happen if changes of management or new con- ditions of trade came into vogue. The employees are, practically without organisation and would in case of need be almost Rowerless. The gas stove trade is many departments worked by unskilled labour with the result that, while boys and young men may get fair wages, there is little prospect in the future for men upon whom a wife and family depend. The disproportionate number of youths employed in the gas stoves and kindred trades here is largelyaccounted for by the weakness of the Unions in this district, and • those who have seen the evil growing have been unable hitherto to arouse the workers to a sense of their danger. It is to be hop3d the members of the Trade Society will redouble their efforts and that greater success will result. The National Union of Stove Grate Workers wisely takes in all grades of men employed 'in the trade, and it and the Scotch Society are on good sound lines. I confess that I view the future position of gas stove workers with considerable alarm and their unpreparedness locally is absolutely suicidal. Organise! Organise ! should be the cry, and if political combination/is accompanied by trades organisation Ido not fear the result. Yours etc

LA.ZARUS. ANSWERS TO CORRESPONDENTS.

F. H.—Christian people cannot conscientiously ignore the claims of Socialism. They ought not to ignore the teachings of Christ. Jesus Christ taught Communism which is "the most advanced form of the policy gener- ally known as Socialism." If Communism is distasteful to you, how can you honestly call yourself a follower of Christ ?

LIFE IN THE WORKHOUSE. The Brabazon Scheme.

THE SHIP CANAL BILL.

(Ctintiteaea frsr. Page 3). WALTON SLUICES GUARDED.

The dispute between the Corporation and the Man- chester Ship Canal Company has taken a curious turn. The Corporation, in conjunction with the traders of the town, are opposing the Bill under which the Canal Com- pany seek further pumping powers, by which it is alleged, the salt water will reach the Mersey at War- rington from the canal. As the manufacturers take water from the river for boiler and other purposes, it is held that these powers may seriously affect the trade of Warrington by forcing the firms to seek a new water supply. As a counter-stroke the Company claim that they already have power to open sluices gatesat Walton and they have recently done this. The Corporation have gone to law over the point, and in the meantime have stationed a body of men at the sluices to prevent the Company's men from opening them at high tide. The Corporation force is the larger one, and so far has succeeded in holding the sluices.

Opposition was ()tiered to the Manchester Ship Canal Company's (Various Powers) Bill before the Select Committee of the House of Commons yesterday. Mr. Honoratus Lloyd, counsel for the Canal company, suggested that the incre3se in salinity could only amount to 11 grains of salt per gallon. To this, Mr. Priest, engineer, of Liverpool, who was giving evidence on behalf of the Warrington traders replied, "I don't think the petitioners would be here for a grain of salt. You are here for powers to do something which has frightened the petitioners, and they don't want to be damaged."

THE HORSE PARADE, CREDITABLE DISPLAY AT

lii'AririlliUTON.

It was fortunate that the weather on Saturday after- noon was more in harmony with the traditions of the "merry month," for it ensured the success of Warrington's seventh annual horse parade, in which the townspeople take a great interest. The display was a highly creditable one, although in point of numbers there was a slight falling off, and showed that the object of the Committee —to encourage drivers and owners to take a greater pride in their horses—is being achieved. There was a splendid array of horse- flesh, and as the well-kept, smartly groomed animals passed along the crowded streets many expressions of admiration were heard. Warrington has indeed every reason to be proud that it can muster such a fine exhibition' and we hope the parade, which has now become firmly established, will long continue to fulkl its admirable purpose. As usual the competitors assembled in Arpley fields where the judging began at noon. Sir Gilbert Greenall, :Bart, the popular president, showed his interest in the parade by attending and he was accompanied by Mr. William Fletcher and Councillor J. Fulton-Smith, his fellow-members of the Reception Committee. So uniformly high was the quality of the exhibits that -the judging was by no means easy. It was, however, efficiently accomplished to the satisfaction of all, and at the close the prizes and diplomas were distribUted by Mrs. W. P. Rylands. This pleasing little ceremony over, Sir Gilbert Greenall proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs. Itylands and briefly alluded to the improvement in the parade which had been witne:sed during the past few years. It was evident that the horses were very well cared for all the year round. Mr. W. Fletcher seconded and expressed the hope that in future more ladies would show their interest in the. parade by attending. The vote was heartily accorded, three dicers being given for Mrs.Rylands.ln responding Mrs Rylands said she very much regretted the absence of Lady Greenall who took such a keen interest in the proceedings. The procession then moved off, headed, by the Borough Fire Bridage, under the command of Super- intendent Turner, with their gaily-decorated engine. Then came Walker's band playing lively selections, and the magnificent teams of horses belonging to the firm followed. An equally attractive display was provided by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. whose fire brigade and large number of powerful horses figured prominently. The Warrington Slate Co., as usual, showed many horses in splendid condition and secured the medal for the best pair of animals in -the procession. Messrs. Crosfield's fire brigade looked very smart and the rest of the procession was made up of a long line of turnouts of various classes. More music was idiscoursed by the Borough Band. Most of the horsed were gaily caparisoned and some of the owners must have gone to considerable trouble and expense to secure pleasing decorative effects. The only feature which could be termed a I novelty was the turnout of Messrs. Crawford, the well known biscuit manufacturers, whith obtained a I silver cup at Liverpool. A lurry drawn by a splendid horse, was surmounted with a unique arrangement of biscuit boxes towering high in the air and forming I a conspicuous object which attracted general attention. The procession traversed the following route :—Parker- street, Sankey-street, Buttermarket-street, Church- street, round the lamp, Church-street, Mersey-street, Bridge-street, Horsemarket-street, Winwick-street, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane, Priestley-street, Powys- , street, Bank Quay, Sankey-street, Bold-street, Wilson Patten-street. Praise is due to the officials for the admirable arrangements and especially to Mr: A. H. Clive and Councillor Fulton Smith wbo very ably 'discharged the duties of hon. secretary and hon. treasurer respectively The other officers were : —Committee :—Messrs. W. Bainbridge, H. Ferguson, jun., W. Fletcher, Robert Garton, S. Pickton, H. G. Roberts, W. P. Rylands, R. Spann, George Venn; judges :—Messrs. John Abbott, Thomas Bexter, Wm. Fairclough, Peter Knowles, John jAngshaw, George Marsh. Jos. Percival, N. Pilling, E. W. Sankey, Robert Turner, J. Turton, W. Gilbert, Jas. Gould ' • Chief Marshal: Mr. T. H. Pretty; referee, Mr. James Gould ; assistant marshal, Mr. Herbert Burrows ; stewards, Messrs. W. P. Rylands, R. Spann, W. Fletcher, W. Tinsley, R. Garton, S. mown, H. G. Roberts, W. THE CHURCHES .AND MODERN THOUGHT. Messrs. Watts S: Co. have now issued a second and revised edition of "The Churches and iliodern Thought." This work is attracting considerable attention. The first edition, published last year, consisted of one thousand copies—the present •edition comprises five thousand copies ; but the publishers confidently expect that another and still larger edition will be required at no distant date. The book, both in its style and in its subject-matter, appeals to the widest possible range of readers, and, to render its purchase within the means of a large circle, a popular edition is being issued con- currently with the new library edition. The publishers believe that a work dealing exhaustively, temperately, And lucidly with the arguments for and against belief, and also stimulating to thouseht in vital and healthy ways, is a want much felt at this present time of religious unrest, and that the appearance of such a book at the psychical moment could hardly have been more opp_ortune._

-Messrs. Watts are issuing, for the Rationalist Press Association a sixpenny edition of Francis William New:uan's "Phases of Faith." Mr. A. W. Senn con- tributes an appreciative introduction, in which he alludes to the author's simple-minded sincerity and "lucid, impressive, unaffected language," and characterises the work as "the record of a brave thinker's inner life." Another work, re-issued for the above Association at sixpence, is Mrs. C. P. Gilman's "Concerning Children," an original and sympathetic study of childhood and its true training by the gifted author of " Women and Economics." ro this repdnt a brief introduction is supplied by Miss Margaret McMillan. "A Hundred Years of Education Con- troversy "is the title of a pamphlet by Mr. J. McCabe, also issued by Messrs. Watts. It contains a brief historical review of the struggle in England between the Established Church and the Nonconformists as to the religious education to be given in state-sided or supported schools. Mr. McCabe maintains that the only solution of the difficulty is to confine national education to secular subjects, ,leaving the churches to provide outside the schools what religious instruction may be desired by the parents.

" LEA-FRANCIS," "PREMIER," and " BUDGE- ,' WHITWORTH " CYCLES. Price from .C 4 10s. Cash or easy payments.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. A Cheshire Walk Thrown Open. Lord Delamere has decided to throw open the new Park Road, Vale Royal, to pedestrians, if unaccom- panied by dogs, between 0 a.m. and sunset. This is the road to secure which the Northwich Rural Council fought Lord Delamere in the law courts. The High Court held that Lord Delamere had absolute rights. The road has since been closed. It is one of the most delightful walks in Cheshire. Lord Delamere appeals to the public to respect the privilege and to keep to the roadway, which is bounded on either side by woods and game covers. Child's Narrow Escape. A young child had a very narrow escape from serious injury on Monday. About 1.30 William Ellison, in the employ of the Wigan Coal and Iron Company was driving a horse attached to a coal lurry along Knutsford-road when Sarah Harper, aged one year and ten months, daughter of Peter Harper, 35, Old-road, stepped off the footpath near the corner of School-street and was knocked down by one of the wheels of the vehicle. Her ankle was crushed but fortunately her injuries were not of a more serious character. After receiving attention at the Infirmary the little girl was taken home. LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Adtv. Junior Conservative Drive. After postponement on account of rain, the first evening drive in connection with the Junior Conservative Association and Club took place on Tuesday, the route being through Grappenhall and Lymm. 'Buses to the number of nine, conveyed the party, numbering about 400, leaving the club in Orford-lane shortly after half-past six. Among those present were Councillor Fulton Smith (deputy cb airman of the Conservative Association), Mr. Edward Draper (chairman of the Junior Conservative Club), Mr. Herbert Hayes (organizing secretary), and Mr. W. MCNiel Sloane. A short halt was made at Lymm for refreshments, after which a start was made for home which was reached by way of Statham, Thelwall and Latchford, at 9.50, all having greatly enjoyed the outing. Water Polo. Warrington polo team journeyed to Hyde on Tues- day evening when they met the Hyde Seal team. The game was hotly contested from the outset, and War- rington were the first to score through W. Ashton, the Cheshire county man. Hyde retaliated, and G. Wilkinson soon equalized. The same player added a second goal before the interval, and ends were changed, Hyde leading 2-1. In the second half Hyde were the first to attack, and the Hyde captain beat Woods. The visitors now attacked spiritedly, and Allen beat G. Wilkinson with a long s,hot. After this Hyde held the upper hand and goals were added by Lister and Wilkinson. Result : Hyde Seal, 5 ; Warrinal—n 2.

Serious Gas Explosion. A. serious gas explosion occurred in the Penketh shop of the Warrington Co-operation Society, on Tues- day. It appears that one of the assistants, named William Pickton Tucker opened the shop and per- ceived a strong smell of gas. He at once proceeded to investigate with a lighted taper with the result that there was immediately an expbsion.The windows were blown out, and fragments of glass were flung across the road. Tucker shouted for help, and John Tunstall, who happened to be in the stable yard at the hack of .the the shop, heard his call, and burst the door open, , finding him on his back quite unconscious and in ' great danger of suffocation. A man, named Marshall Whitfield, who was standing outside near the window when the explosion occured received cuts on his hands from the broken glass. Tucker was attended by Dr. IMurray and is recovering rapidly from the severe shock. PUBLIC MWEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEiT BOOTS IN L ANCASHIRE.—AdVt. Primrose League Meeting. The annual general meeting of the Daresbury Prim- rose League, Habitation No. 2,058, was held on Tuesday night in the Sessions Room, Daresbury. Mr. I. I. Wynne-Jones, ruling councillor, presiding. The Hon. Treasurer (Mr. J. E. Seeker) submitted the balance sheet, which showed that the year was started with a balance in hand of £ll us. 3d., and ended with £l2 3s. sd. in hand. The Ruling Councillor remarked that the habitation was self- supporting, not withstanding the entertainment given during the winter. Considering what had been done the balance sheet was very creditable. The Ruling Councillor proposed the re-election of Lady Greenall as Dame President. Mr. Houghton seconded, and the motion was cordially passed. Mr. Cross proposed the re-election of Mr. Wynne-Jones as ruling councillor The motion was unanimously agreed to. Mr. Seeker was re-elected hon. treasurer. Mr. Phillips hon. secretary and the following were re-elected members of the Executive Council: Colonel Ridgway and Messrs. Frank Tinsley, C. Shepherd Cross, Frank Birch, W. Fowles, Edward de Prez, A. da Prez, H. Tickle, W. Weir.

Metal Thefts. A sequel the case in which a man named Charles Daniels last week was sentenced to three months' imprisonment for stealing brass and iron from the Warrington District Threshing Company's 'Yard, Lythgoe's-lane, was heard at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday. Daniels made an accusation again 9t Robert Flatman, Ellesmere-street, employed by the Threshing Company,who was a witness against him,and Flatman was now charged with stealing a vice value 305., the property of the Company, and a quantity of iron, belonging to the National Telephone Company. Reading a report of the Daniels case a toolmaker named James Matthew Molyneux, Ellesmere-street, who bought a 'vice from Flatman for four shillings, came forward and was the principal witness against him. Flatman was sentenced to four months' hard labour.—The Bench said they were extremely dis- satisfied with Molyneux's condnut. He ought to have known that Flatman could not have come by the vice , honestly to sell it for four shillings. Molyneux's expenses would be disallowed. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER ME CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN la ANCASHIRE.—Advt. Board of Guardians. A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday under the presidency of Mr. A. J. Jolley. 'There were also present :—Mrs. Wilkin- son, Mrs. Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. M. Ryan, Dr. Murray, Messrs.E. L.Bent,A.Brooks, W. C.Brooks, S. Davies, W. Flanagan, R. Forshaw, W. Furness, G. Mather, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. A.wbery (master) and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate schools)—The Clerk said the audit only finished on Tuesday evening, having lasted practically a week and all the books and papers had been in the procession of the'auditor until now, with the result that he (Mr. Sutton) had no work to bring before the Board. When he was • appointed clerk the audit used to last a little over one day, and at that time it was sufficient for the audit of all the parishes in the Union.—Mr. Brdoks asked if the auditor would report to the Board. —The Clerk said that as a rule the report was sent to the Rural District Council. He had a two hours' dis- cussion with the audor as to whether a certain item of expenditure should be surcharged on the Council or not, but he was pleased to say that after consulting the law books and authorities, the auditor decided not to surcharge.—lt was reported that the number of inmates in the House last Friday was 493, as compared with 483 for the corresponding day last year. The vagrants relieved during the week numbered 269 as against 352.

It is very seldom that a great man when be departs this life leaves behind him a great son. The late Sir William Harcourt, M.P., made a great name in the political arena. He did noble work in his day, the greatest feat being his " Death Duties Budget," whereby he showed the nation how to obtain revenue without pressing Upon the necessities of life, or further taxing the people's food. His son, the present Member for the Rossendale diVision of Lancashire, if he con- tinues as he is now going on, will make a big name for himself in the country. The manner in which he coached through the House of Commons his " 'sine Man, One Vote" Bill, was simply splendid. Pity, indeed it was, that all the time devoted to that measure was lost, and the Bill itself destroyed by that wretched institution, the House of Lords. If the working men of this country would only give a tithe of the attention to politics which they do give to matters of much less importance, this lordly institution would soon be set in order. Having lost his first Bill, Mr. Harcourt, nothing daunted, returns to the attack. Once more he storms the citadel of monopoly and privilege with his "Small Holdings Bill." The land question is a great I problem. The difficulties of our present system of land tenure cannot be overcome by one legislative enactment. It has three chief aspects : rural, urban, and town aspects. The latter perhaps is at the present time most acute. Mr. Harcourt tackles the rural side of the question. Be seems to recognize that the land is God's free gift to the whole human race; that rural land is of no use unless it is cultivated ; that the cultivation of land can only properly be carried out by people who understand the business ; and that it is the duty of the State to encourage the cultivation on these lines. The Small Holdings Bill is a capital measure as it stands at present. The newspapers tell us that the Labour members in the Rouse are delighted with it, and we all know that the member for Warrington will give it an enthusiasic support. First, it makes no provision for peasant ownership. I believe that individual ownership of land is generally a mistake, whether the owner be peer or peasant. The land should be for the use of the people, and the right to properly use it belongs as much to unboen generations as to the people of this present time. The definition of the word allotment" is in the future to mean from one to five acres, and the small holding will go from 5 acres to 50. The local governing body of the distriot is to have the power to acquire land, either by voluntary arrange- ment or by compulsory purchase, but no additional price is to be paid on account of the compulsion. Land lying idle outside towns can be acquired by the local councils, and held at a fair agricultural rent, to be released again when required for building or indus- trial purposes. It would perhaps have been as well if the purchase price of this land had been fixed by the Bill, at its present rateable value, for it is a curious thing how little some land is worth when it is to be assessed for rating purposes, and how enormously and rapidly it increases in value when it is wanted for building purposes. Mr. Harcourt's Bill also gives the local authorities power to acquire grazing land, and sheep walks for attachment to small holdings in common. Co-opera- tive credit banks are to be established to advance small loans to tenants, and the local authorities are to be allowed to borrow money for the purpose of pur- chasing, the repayments being spread over a period of 80 years. The authority has also power to transfer land to associations of small holders, subject to the approval of the Board of Agriculture and this board is permitted to set up experimental small holdings in order to demonstrate their feasibility, the Treasury making a grant of £lOO,OOO for this purpose. The holders are to be occupying,cultivating tenants, with security of tenure and full compensation for unexhausted improvements when they give up posses- sion of their holding. One of the most important provisions of the Bill is that where any authority refuses to do its duty, the Board of Agriculture will appoint Commissioners to do it for them, and charge any expenses so incurred against the defaulting authority. Bravo Harcourt! It is a splendid attempt to tackle a big question, and it will be interesting now to watch the kind of reception this Bill meets with, from various sections of the ;political world. "As a landlord myself,' said Mr. Harcourt, "I welcome the compulsion which is comprised in the Bill. I stand in a position very common among my class. lam a tenant for life of my estate under a very strict settlement entail. I have no son ; I am therefore compelled, or at all events am morally bound, and so are my `ne ultimate remainaer umn,who may be a distant relative, and to exercise a strictness of administration and absence of social and political bias which I should not feel myself compelled to exercise if I had a son or was the owner in fee simple. I therefore welcome this compulsion, not that it will be necessary to put it in - operation against myself, but because it will enable me and my trustees to act voluntarily and by agreement in a way in which nothing else could have done."

Noble sentiments these, I wonder how many Tory landlords will endorse them? The vultures, I am afraid, will soon begin to close round this tempting morsel. We are sure to hear a lot about "Confiscation," and the" Sacred rights of property," but the Liberals must look after the more sacred rights of humanity. What will the House of Lords say to a Bill like this.? They would sooner put a tax on corn than provide agricultural holdings for the people. They may not have the courage to reject this Bill, but the people must narrowly watch how the noble Lords propose to "Amend it." Those who live on rents will not be very enthusiastic on behalf of this Bill. In the Chartist days a Radical poet wrote the following. "For what were all these landed patriots born? To hunt, and vote, and raise the price of corn. Safe in their barns, these Sabine tillers sent Their brethren out to battle. Why? For rent!

Year after year they voted cent per cent, Blood, sweat, and tear wrung millions. Why? For rent! They roared,they dined,they drank,they swore,they meant To die for England. Why then live? For rent! And will they not repay the treasure lent? . .

No! down With everWling, and np with rent! Their good, ill, health, wealth, joy, or discontent, Being, end, aim, religion—rent, rent, rent !" These words could be applied to some of the Tory members in the House of Lords at the present time. , I often wonder what the Tory workingman thinks of a struggle such as the one we shall be engaged in during the next few weeks in reference fo Mr. Harcourt's measure. Everyone knows how the depopulation of our rural districts continues, and how acute becomes the struggle for existence in our large towns, because of the exodus from our villages. Why should so much of our own land lay idle, while we have to send abroad for our food, part of which might be grown at home. It is a great blessing that our ports are open, anti that we can obtain what we require so easily and cheaply from other lands; but that is no excuse for allowing British acres to remain uncultivated, and all working men and women, whether they be Liberal or Tory, ought to hail with enthusiasm any man who like Mr. Harcourt is endeavouring to restore the people to the land and give us an opportunity of diminishing poverty and finding profitable occupation for some of the unemployed. My advice to the neople at the present time is watch narrowly this interesting struggle over Harcourt's Bill ; take note of what is said and done by the opponents of the measure, keep your eyes on the House of Lords, and remember in reference to "all whom it may concern" " By their fruits ye shall know them."

THE WHITE CITY.

HALE'S TOURS OF THE WORLD.

I Hale's Tours of the world, this grand demonstrator of the possibilities of the world, as well as a national educator of man, is testing the capacity of its two ' handsome Pullman Cars at the White City daily. These tours are not only very interesting but instruc- tive. It is a colossal object lesson and exceptionally different from all other exhibitions, and does not in any degree rely for its success upon the display of sentimental features that have no utility. So success- ful have Hale's Tours of the World been in London that the Company have established branches at the White City, Manchester, Blackpool, Nottingham, Leeds, Now Brighton, Liverpool, Bristol, Dublin, Glasgow, Swansea and Cardiff, while others will be opened as quickly as cars can be built.

At the Egyptian Hall of the London Mansion House on Friday there was a large and distinguished gather- in the occasion of the presentation to Sir John Kirk (who on the previous day had unexpectedly received the honour of knighthood at the hands of the King) of the national testimonial which had been raised in recognition of his work in connection with the Ragged School Union. The testimonial included a cheque for £2,000, a portrait of Sir John painted in oils (which is to be hung in the Council Room of the Ragged School Union), and an illuminated address. A replica of the portrait, and a drawing-room clock with Westminster chimes, were presented to Lady Kirk.

The Lancashire Education Co mmittee met at Preston on Monday, Eir Henry F. Hibbert presiding. Mr. S. Taylor congratulated Sir Henry outlaying been placed on the Consultative Committee of the Board of Edacation, which was almost the Blue Riband for those interested in education, and Sir Henry, in reply, said he hoped he would have time and health to do his duty both to the Cummittee and to the county. Sir Henry Hibbert, in moving the adoption of the report of the Higher Education Sub-Committee, said that body had gone very carefully into the services and individual merits of their respective teachers. He thought the result of the work would be to give justice to their assistant teachers in the day secondary schools. At any rate, he took it that the reason the had raised the salaries of the assistant masters was that there should be some inducement for men offiintelligence and ability to enter the secondary schools of the county. With regard to prizes for evening schools and classes, it was now recommended that the sum to be included in the estimates for a district in any one year should not exceed five per cent: of the grant received from the Board of Education during the financial year imme- diately' preceding on behalf of the schools and classes of that district. In his opinion, that was a wise regula- tion. During 1905 and 1906 there was spent on prizes and prize distribution a sum of about £2,380, and for 1906-7 there was included in the estimates a sumlof £2,262. By the new rule the money voted for this purpose would be reduced to £1,150. They had, there- fore, not only reduced the amount of money given by the ratepayers for prizes, but they had instituted a kind of promotion by merit or payment by result, which ought to act advantageously throughout the entire area.

ILIOUS NO MO' E.

A FARMER'S LIFE-LONG SUFFERING FROM BILIOUSNESS, CONSTIPATION, HEADACHES AND DIZZINESS.

HOW HE WAS CURED AT LAST BY MOTHER SEIOEL'S SYRUP.

The remedy that can cure biliousness, in one who has 1 suffered with it from infancy to the full prime of early manhood, must be a real remedy, and such is Mother Seigel's Syrup. Here is the proof of what yr e say. Mr Herbert Shipley, a young married farmer, of Scawcett Farm, Epworth, by Doncaster, in a statement, dated January 3rd, 1907, says : "All my life I have been subject to biliousness. I had bilious attacks when a boy at school, and as I grew up they got worse., I have had as many as three dsitinct attacks in a fortnight. They came on with headache and dizziness, I could not eat, and s felt thoroughly wretched. I was nervous, too, and often when out dtiving I hardly knew how to get out of the way of carts or traps I met.. Always the attack would work up, getting worse and worse, till it ended in vomiting. I used to heave till I could hardly sta.nd,till I felt like sinking into my boots, as I used to say. Then when this was over, I would have a little peace for a time, till the next attack came on. At all times I was plagued with constipation, for which there seemed no real relief.

"So things went on till, ab out a year ago, I was advised te take Mother Seigel's Syrup. I felt bette almost from the first dose, though then ii the middle of an attack, and soon I was quite well. At the present time I am in thorough good health."

This is a message of hope for the bilious. H owever long you may have suffered, how- ever severely, Mother Seigel's Syrup will cu re you Take it, and cease to suffer.

COUNTY EDUCATION COMMITTEES.

LANCASHIR?..

CHESHIRE.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee on Monday, Dr. Hodgson, who presided, alluded to Mr. MoKenna's scheme of regulations affecting grants to secondary schools, and expressed his fears that those regulations might seriously retard secondary education in Cheshire. Instead of offering relief to the rate- payers he thought the effect of the regulations might mean a great addition to the rates. It was proposed to have two scales of 'grants, and that •to earn the higher grant they would have to have at least twevi,r. eve per cent. OL -----,uation free. He viewed the proposals with great alarm as calculated to waste an enormous amount of public money without any profitable return. In Cheshire they had laid down a system of education embracing all types without respect to social status and it seemed to him that a sudden revolutionary Change like this, without reference to the feelings of Local Authorities, would be a calamity. (Hear, hear.) A deputation was appointed to interview the Board of Education on the subject. _ _

Tile meeting re-affirmed by a large majority its con- viction, on the motion of Mr. J. J. Evans, that school attendance summonses should be served by the police and not by school attendance officers • and also, on the motion of Judge Bradbury, seconded by Dr. Hewitt, decided to report to the Board of Education that there was no necessity for putting the Provision of Meals Act into operation in Cheshire for the present. The Director of Education drew attention to the fact that tke Government grants on account of elementary schools were liable to decline on account of the absence from school of children suffering from infectious sick- ness.

Mr. T. Beeley complained that some of their schools were simply poison dens where children were crowded to the prejudice of their health. When they were poisoning the children it was no wonder they had epidemics. _ _ _ _ _

'After discussion it was decided to ask the Board of Education to compensate the authorities for any loss of grant in such cases.

WELCOME TO Agnes Blythe, Gladys Hooper, Deborah Cope, Benny Farrar, John L. Watts.

_ giant once lived in a large and grafter estate. He used to go round to poor farmers' houses and steal a sheep .to eat, as he did not want. to kill his own sheep. A poor widow lived in the neighbourhood, who had but one son to help her. This boy was a great 1p to his mother. When his father was alive, he had bought the boy a lamb, to which he was very, kind. This lamb did the giant cove', Ile stole into the yard one night, and took the lamb. Then a lovely fairy 'appeared before the boy, who said, "That great, ugly giant has stolea your lamb, so,, as you have been a good boy to loth your mother and the lamb, here is a magic sword. Go, follow the giant, slay him, get your lamb back again, and then call for me, and I will tell you what to do." So the boy went, caught up to the giant, and quite took him by surprise. Ile climbed up his back, drew the magic sword, and cut off the giant's head. He fell to the ground, dead. The boy got his lamb back, and then the fairy appeared before him again. She said, "All the giant's estate now belongs to you. Go, bring your mother, and live in the giant's castle." The boy returned the sword to the fairy, and said "No, thank you; my mother would have too much work to do if she had to keep a whole giant's castle clean." The fairy nodded in approval, and vanished into the night.

BENNY fAliltilt.

OOMPLAINTS.

Too fat, too lean, too hot, too cold, I always do complain; Too raw, too roast, too young, too old— Faults I will find or feign. Let it be flesh, or fowl, or fish, It never shall be said But I'll find fault with meat or dish,

With master or with maid. But when I go to bed at night, I heartily do weep, That I must part with my delight— I cannot scold the sleep. However, this doth mitigate And much abate my sorrow. That though to-night it be too late, I'll early scold to-morrow. JOHN THOMAS HOPSON.

THE WAY HE HEARD IT.

A sailor who had been to a church service when he heard some fine music, was afterwards descanting upon an anthem which had given him great pleasure. A listening shipmate finally asked: "I say, Bill, what's a hanthem 7" " What!" exclaimed Bill. "Do you mean to say you don't know what a hanthem is?" "Not me."

"Well, then, I'll tell yer. If I was to tell yer, 'Ere, Bill, give me that 'andspike,' that wouldn't be a hanthem. But if I was to say, 'Bill. Bill, Bill, give, give, give me, that, Bill, give me, give me that 'and, give me that 'and, . 'andspike, spike, spike, Bill, give, me that, that, 'and, 'andspike, 'and, 'andspike, spike, spike, spike. Ah-men, ah-men. Billgivemethat'and- spike, spike. Ahmen 1' why, that would be a hanthem 1" AGgES SMITE.

ONE WAS MISSING.

Two men once stopped at a French inn, and gave in charge of the landlady, who was a widow, a bag of money, telling her to give it up to neither of them, unless they were both to- gether. A little while afterwards one of the men came alone, and asked the landlady to give up the money, under the pretence that his com- panion had to make an important payment im- mediately. The widow had paid little attention to what had been said to her before, and now, forgetting all about it, gave up the bag. The rogue disappeared with it so quickly that the landlady asked herself if she had not made a mistaka the next day the other man turned up, and vnade the same request as his comrade had done,the previous day, and when the widow told him what had happened, he went into a passion, and summoned her for the loss of his money. Someone, who heard of the poor woman's plight, advised her to say that she was ready to bring forth the money on the original terms. She asked the plaintiff to produce his comrade. The argument was found plausible by the Court, and as the thief took care not to come back, his comrade had to give up his elaina. DAISY STOCKS.

SHE WAS BUSY.

A lady came down from upstairs and asked the manager of the hotel if she could get a glass of water at orce._ _ _

Why, certainly, madam," said the manager, filling up a glass from the water-cooler. Two minutes later she was back in the office again. "I don't like to trouble you." she said, "but could I get another glass of water right away V" "No trouble at all, madam," said the manager, handing her another glass. . .

===== 0002601_19070601_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
Two- Minutes liter she appeared again. This time she asked for two glasses. "Certainly, madam," said the affable manager, "but could I inquire what you are doing with so much water?" "Well," she said, "I don't want to frighten you, but my room is on fire, and, I'm trying to put it out.' Lucy MURRELL.

THE BUTTER THAT SPED THE GUESTS.

"Ma wants two pcunds of butter exactly like what you sent us last. If it ain't exactly like that she won't take it," said the small boy. The grocer turned to his numerous cus- tomers, and remarked blandly: "Some people In my businesTdon't like particular customers, but I do. It's my delight to serve them end get them what they want; I will attend to You in a moment, little boy." "Be sui. 0.,,u get me same kind," said the small boy, while the storeful of customers listened to him. "A lot of pa's relations are visiting our house, and ma doesn't want 'em to come again." PERCY BAXTER.

PAT'S BEST MAN.

' Pat was invited 0 a wedding. He arrived at the house faultlessly attired in full evening dress, a huge white chrysanthemum adorning his buttonhole. He was shown upstairs to the gentlemen's dressing-room. The guests assembled below were suddenly startled by hearing a great commotion above. Rushing into the hall to ascertain the cause, they were somewhat startled to behold Pat come tumbling head first down the stairs, cora- gdetely dishevelled. Upon the amazed host exclaiming: "Why, Pat, what is the matter?" Pat answered: • ,

"Shure and I wint upstairs, and whin I wint inter the room I seed a swell young dandy wid a white earnationarymum in hit buttonhole and kid gloves on his hands, an' I sez to 'im, 'Who's you Shure,' he sez, 'an' I'm the best Lan'; and, begorry, he is." R. I. C.

THE HAPPIEST LAND. There sat one day in quiet, By an alehouse on the Rhine, Your hale and hearty fellows, And drank the precious wine. The landlord's daughter filled their cups Around the rustic board; Then sat they all So calm and still,

_ And spake not one rude word. But when the maid departed, _ _ _

A Swabian raised his hand (Then sat they all so calm and still), And cried, all hot and flushed with wine, "Long live the Swabian land! The greatest kingdom upon earth Cannot with that compare, With all the stout and hardy men, And the nut-brown maidens there." "Ha 1" cried a Saxon, laughing, And dashed his beard with wine, "I had rather live in Lapland Than that Swabian land of thine!" The goodliest land on all this earth, It is the Saxon land!

SOMETHING FATHER COULD NOT DO. Sometimes on a Saturday afternoon, When Father from work is free, We all of us play a glorious, game— We call it "Menagerie." Then Algernon James is a tiger bold, And a monkey is Marjorie, And Tom on the floor is a great big whale, Floundering in the sea. Then Father growls like a grizzly bear, And roars like a lion, too: Sometimes he whistles, just like a bird, Or squeals like a cockatoo. But Father didn't know what to do— tie could only sit and smile— ". When Algernon said, "Oh, Father, do Make a noise like a crocodile." MAGGIE PATRICK.

KINDNESS.

See 1 George has a fine new hat. What has he done with his old one? I will tell you. On the day the new one came home, the rain fell very, fast, and he saw a poor boy in the street, whose clothes were torn, and the rain fell very much on his bare head. His face was wet and pale, and George saw that he was ill. So George told his friends, who gave him leave- to do what he thought best. Then George gave the poor boy his old hat and an old coat, and some bread and cheese, and his friends were glad to see that he was so kind and irnod.

LILY BOGG.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l should so like to be one of your Nephews, as I read your Corner every week. I am sending you a bit of poetry, named, "King Arthur's Servants," and this is it: When good King Arthur first began to wear long, hanging sleeves, he entertained three starving men. And all of them were thieves. The first was an Irishman; the second was a Scot; the third was a Welshman—three merry rogues, I wot. The Irishman was burned; the Scot was drowned in ale; and the Welshman was likely to be choked by a *rat, but they pulled it out by the tail. THOMAS SIMMS.

THE NAUGHTY FINGERS

Oh, I'll tell you a story that nobody knows, Of ten little fingers and ten little toes, Of two pretty eyes and one little nose, And where they all went one day. Oh, the .little round nose smelled someihiss sweet, Something that surely was nice to eat; And patter away went two little feet, Out of the room one day. Ten little toes climbed up on a chair, Two eyes peeped over a shelf, and there Lay a lovely cake, all frosted and fair, Made by Mamma that day.
