SCIENCE NOTES AND'NEWS.

IS THERE WATER ON THE MOON?

Some striking photographs made by Pro- fessor W. H. Pickering ,of the volcanoes in the, Hawaiian Islands serve to point out cer- tain characteristics which they have in common with the craters on the moon, says the Scientific American. One photograph of a long crack, extending some miles, in the lava crust, at 'Hawaii serves to .emphasise Professor Pickering's belief that water, or water vapour exists on the moon, and by irrigating cracks on the moon's surface gives rise to vegetation in them, just as trees and, shrubs have sprung up in the *Hawaiian lava crack. In studying Erasto- thenes in 1904, :_Professor Pickering found its interior seamed with numerous fine cracks. Watching some of these cracks soon after the sun arose on them he was able to see them broaden out and change gradually into canals. It is his belief that the cracks gave out water vapour, which fertilised the vegetation along their sides and in their neighbourhood,. and that it was the growth of this vegetation which produced the ap- pearance of a canal. A further inference is that the chnals on Mars, which become znore clearly visible at seme periods of the year, owing to the melting of the Martian polar ice cap and the flooding of the waterways, are similar cracks on the surface of Mars. Cracks of the kind occur oia the moon. The largest of them is that known as Sirsalis, which is 400 Miles in length.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RED MOON.

We all, know the astronomical charac- teristics of the "blue moon," but a good deal of uncertainty exists as to the signifi- cance and atmospheric influence of the "red moon." To rustics it is a synonym for the bad weather of early cpring, and as there is a vast amount of empiric experience em- bodied in these folk proverbs, it would be in- teresting if some astronomer would kindly tell u.s just how much or how little there really is of scientific basis for the bucolic antipathies against the "red moon." _ If our satellite, like the dog with a bad name, could be hanged, the moon at its " red " phase of the spring would have been hanged ages ago. For the peasant, the ex- pression red moon" signifies that cold snap with its attendant chills, miseries, and depressions, which usually follows rudely and suddenly upon the first few balmy days of mild spring weather.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
EVAPORATION OF METALS.

Experiments carried out by Zengheli, and othrrs, have shewn that evaporation takes place from 'the surface of solid metals and their compounds without the aid of heat. An interesting method of proving this consists in suspending a piece of chemically pure silver over the metal in an air-tight vessel. The evaporated metal is taken up awl re- tained by the silver. After an experiment with zinc, for example, says the Glob!, analysis shewed 2 per cent. of this metal in the silver. In most cases, however, the silver .is simnly weighed, and the increase in weight shews. tlic amount of metallic vapour absorbed. Abkmption uf alkali metals is proved by flame tests, and of copper iron by colour reactions. In the case of sulphides the action on the silver indi- cates that the metal and the sulphur are separated in evaporation. The metals pro- bably form an alloy with the silver. It is thought by some that there is a connection between such evaporation of metals and their effect on photographic plates. The photographic effect may, in fact, be du c to the particles of the metals which are stream- ing off. Streintz suggests that the evapora- tion is due to what is known as electrolytic pressure. Positive ions, that is, atoms or molecules with a charge of positive elec- tricity, are given off. These affect the photographic plate, ionise the air—that is, electrify its particles---and leave the metal negatively electrified. Thus the phenomena of evaporation of solid metals, power of effecting the photographic plate, and radio- activity seem closely connected, and are common to many metals and their compounds.

WHEN THE EARTH WILL BE FULL. Professor Ravenstein . has estimated that the fertile lands of the globe amount to 28,000,000 square miles, the steppes to 14,000,000, and the deserts to 1,000,000. Fix- ing 207 persons to the square mile for fertile lands, 10 for steppes, and 1 for deserts as the greatest population that the earth could properly nourish, the professor arrives at the conclusion that when the number of inhabi- tants reaches about 6,000,000,000, the earth will be peopled to its full capacity. At pre- sent, says the New York Tribune, it contains somewhat more than one-quarter of that number. If the rate of increase shewn by the latest census statistics should be uni- formly maintained, Professor Ravenstein shews that the globe would be fully peopled about the year 2072.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
WONDERS OF A WATCH.

A watch is the smallest, most delicate machine that was ever constructed of the same number of parts. About 175 different pieces of material enter into its' cconstruc- tion, and upwards of 2,400 separate opera- tions _are . _comprised. :a its manufacture. When considered in the aggregate, some of the facts connected with its. pedoi.upeca.._ appear incredible. A blacksmith • strikes several thousand blows on his anvil in a day, but the roller jewel of a watch makes every clay, and day after day, 432,000 im- pacts against the fork. However, the marvel does not stop.. here. it has been estimated that one horse-power would suffice to run 270,000,000 watches.

COLOURED GLASS FOR PLANTS.

One of the devices proposed by an expert of the United States Department of Agricul- ture for improving the growth of plants and fruits is that of placing them under coloured glass, the idea being that light of particular hues is especially favourable to vegetation. The expert referred to has ex- perimented with strawberry plants grown in hothouses -under glass of different colours. His conclusions are interesting. Orange. coloured glass, he says, caused the plants to grow More vigorously, but at the expense of the berries, which were both smaller and later in developing than when the plants were grown under ordinary conditions. Under violet - coloured glass the plants yielded a great quantity of fruit, but it was slow in ripening, small, and of inferior quality. Red, blue, and green glass all proved ininrinhis to the _growth of the plants. The finest berries, . and those which ripened earliest, were grown under plain white glass. It would appear, then, from these experiments that light of different, colours does have differ-mt effect upon vege- tation, as has been claimed; but that, as far as strawberries are concerned, all th% colours that sunlight possesses, when united in ordinary daylight, are more effective in the production...of good fruit than any single colour. Of course, it is not the colour itself, but the character of the light which gives the— colour. that nroduee.% the ef.r.:ct.

The Churches and Temperance.

Sir,—Permit me to call the attention of your readers to the words of the Bishop of Hereford (Dr. Percival) who in his tennal charge to the clergy of his diocese on the 22nd inst. asked "Why the Noncon- formist:ministers were, as a rule, more keen and active in the cause of temperance than the majority of country clergy " and criticised their attitude towards bridge gambling and race meetings." In these out- spoken remarks, I consider he has struck the keynote of what may prove to be a source of great danger, not confined to the diocese of Hereford alone, bat in many other parts of our land. They will, I fear, be specially applicable to the churches of Warrington, Episcopal and Nonconformist alike. Of bridge, gambling and I race meetings I do not speak, but may I say ' that I know of no town where the curse of intemperance is looked upon by the Christian churches with such utter indifference as in Warrington. If this has always been the case I do not wonder that failure has dogged the footsteps of Temperance efforts in bygone days, I have learned, too sadly, that a large number of ministers and professing Christians appear to have no desire to attack the monstrous evil which .saps their congregations and Sunday schools On the other hand I have met with some very favourable exceptions and to these friends I tender my sincere thanks. I wish it to be understood that I have no desire to hurl random charges against any church or individual. But I feel bound to ask why this apathy should exist? It may be urged that Temperance senti- ment is steadily growing. This 'in some degree I I admit is true. I fear, however, that we have a shallow ' foundation upon which to build our future hopes. There are doubtless,many whose hearts are with us. They view with grave concern the influence of the huge liquor traffic and consider their efforts so inadequate that they feel obliged to "give it a miss." But I am surprised to find that at the meeting of the Temperance Legislation League where the 5 per cent. is held out a large proportion of the Christian ministers are in attendance while on the occasion of a Gospel Temper- ance Mission or the visit of Dr. Sheldon these same friends are most conspicuous by their absence. I have every respect, Sir, for all of these friends and I sincerely trust none will misunderstand my attitude. Ido not condemn or judge anyone. But the facts are facing us daily and we can't shut our eyes. The town is becoming gradually degraded and demoralised, towards which drink is the chief contributory cause. Where is there another tpwn of 70,000 inhabitants with- out a Temperance Hail and organised Temperance Council or Society? Sir,we have room for every serious thought and meditation. Legislative efforts will be futile unless we can show the drink fettered inhabitants of the squalid areas that we have a most genuine interest in their welfare. Have they, then, any call upon the Christian Church? Let any professing Christian take note of all he can see or hear on the streets at closing time any Sunday night and I venture to say that he will blush with shame and disgust as he thinks of the attitude of his Church towards this mass of fallen humanity. Fellow workers can nothing be done to uplift these our fallen brethren? Certainly something can be done and must be done. When the Christian Church and individuals stand united it will be accom- plishe,l. "Duty demands it." Can we withhold it? Surely not : Then let us be up and doing. Christian awake and let your watchword be 'For God and humanity." I shall be glad to have the names of all who will become members of a Temperance Council.— Yours &c., Moore, 29th May, 1907.

J. R. GRIFFITHS,

===== 0002601_19070601_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
The Scattered Nation.

Sir,—Next to the affection which all sentient beings .cherish for their offspring, probably the purest natural and abiding feeling of the human heart is the love of home and country. And this beautiful and ennobling -sentiment is strongly and universally cherished in these islands. The people of England and Wales are deeply attached to their respective divisions of the country and, not- withstanding some drawbacks, regard it as the finest and most beautiful country in the world. They are charmed with its mild and genial days, and its glorious moons and starry skies are ever sources of wonder and of joy. To their minds its rural scenes and village homes contain much that is best and purest in the developments of human life. And then is it not the •ancient home of freedom, ever ready and ever willing .to afford refuge and protection to the oppressed of other countries who find their way to its open shores. One ot our poets Byron I think. has. ...Li the "The • cold in clime are cold in blood," and the inhabitants of "Caledonia, stern and wild," must plead guilty to his Lordship's indictment. Their country is indeed ,colder and less genial than the sunny south, and they themselves are somewhat reserved and unemotional. But they have a warm corner in their hearts for their friends, and their attachment to the land of their fathers is proverbial. And when, in some region "far awn," the Scotsman hears the tender melody of "Ye banks and braes o' bonnie Doune" memories of his early home will float through his mind as a beautiful and refresh- ing dream. And the same beaut;ful and abiding love of home and ,country is very strong in our warm-hearted and generous neighbours whose lot is cast in the lovely Island in the West." How many American citizens, born in Ireland, every year incur the cost and brave the dangers and discomforts of an ocean voyage of three or four thousand miles that they may behold once more the lovely cloudland under which they were born, tread again the fields and meadows where they played in the Tears long, long ago, and drop a tear at the shrines and on the graves of their fathers. But, as you know, Mr. Editor, there is a people who must number many millions, who are in the unique posi- tion of having no home or country of their own at present. These people are the lineal descendants of Abraham, the Father of the Faithful and the Friend of God, to whom and to his heirs the land of Canaan was given for an everlasting possession. But Israel only occupied the country—a narrow strip of territory bound- ing the eastern shore of the Mediterranean Sea—for about fifteen hundred years. In the middle of that period the idolatry of" The Golden Calves" led to the carrying away of ten of the tribes into a captivity from which they have never yet emerged. And their final •overthrow as a nation was completed by the Romans more than eighteen hundred years ago. No people were :ever so highly favoured as were the children of Abraham, and no people were ever so severely punished, -for their sins as they were. In the last siege and des- truction of Jerusalem and the Temple, more than a million of them perished by famine and the sword, and the survivors were dispersed into every .country under heaven. One of the most tragic incidents in history marked their last struggle against the Romans. In the Fortress of Masada, on the shore of the Dead Sea, a garrison of nine hundred and sixty persons perished by self-appointed executioners rather than fall alive into the hands of -their enemies ! Their Canaan, once the glory of all lands, now lies waste and desolate under Turkish mis- Tule ; and the site of " the holy and beautiful house " where their fathers worshipped and sang the songs of Zion is now desecrated by the Mosque of Omar ! In one of his Hebrew melodies Lord Byron thus feelingly describes the present condition of this unhappy and, forsaken race : " Tribes of the wandering foot and weary breast, How shall ye flee away and be at rest ! The wild-dove hath its nest, the fox his cave, Mankind their country—lsrael but the grave " And yet wherever the Jews are found, whether in the great centres of population, among the sparser inhabi- tants of the open country, or in the lonely islands of the ocean their thoughts and their hopes are ever turned to Palestine with a devotion and intensity of feeling which we Gentiles fail to realize. For was it not the goodly land, teaming with the choicest productions of nature' into whieh their fathers were divinely led, and where they lived, and were buried in the valley of Jehoshaphat. Its hills and vales were often consecrated by the Seers and Kings whose blessed memories are household words in every British home to-day. And its glorious Temple, once the wonder of the world, was the only one in which the Divine Presence was ever visibly manifested to men!

And very beautiful and comforting is the thought that, however much Israel may have sinned, they are, nevertheless, still beloved for their Father's sake, and a glorious future lies before them. As surely as they were redeemed from the bondage of Egypt so surely will the scattered excites he restored to the promised land, and by the same Almighty power of God. We have prophetic reasons, too, for believing that their second Exodus will be infinitely more important and comprehensive in its results than the first one was. For not only will they themselves experience a great deliverance and a vionderful home-oominw, but through them and by them the Gentile nations will be converted and brought uader the dominion of the "Prince of Peace" who will set up His throne upon 'Mount Zion,and establish a reign of justice and righteousness under the whole heaven. Then violence and oppression will be knows r no more; "Thanksgiving and the voice of melody " will succeed the cries of suffering and sorrow, and the earth and its inhabitants will enjoy a long Sabbath of rest and glory.—Yours truly, May, 1907.

JONA.

WIDNES COUNCILLOR'S DEA.TIL

Widnes readers will hear with regret of the stulden death of Councillor William Norris, at his residence, 104, Derby-road, Farnworth. After ailing for only a week he succumbed to pneumonia late on Monday. Mr. Norris carried on business as a farmer, and hay, straw, and corn dealer, and was well known in the locality. In 1897, on the resignation of Mr. Longton, Mr. Norris was returned unopposed for Farnworth, a position which he held uninterruptedly to the time of his death.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST CENTENARY. • MOW COP CELEBRATIONS.

The Primitive Methodist who had been compelled to delay his visit to the celebrations till Monday last might well be eager for his journey to end, for the morning papers were devoting their columns and illustrations in description of the great doings of the day before. As he journeyed one read that nearly 100,000 visitors had climbed the historic spot on the Sunday alone. Never before have even Primitives gathered in such numbers to the Mecca of their Church. In 1807 when the first Camp Meeting was held 4,000 people were present. Fifty years ago, when the Jubilee of that meeting was celebrated, 50,000 people assembled. It is quite certain that the centenary numbers almost doubled the record. The Rev. A. T. Guttery, of Newcastle-on-Tyne might well say, in speaking from one of the stands on Sunday: " What a sight is this. This is the greatest sight of my life. Oh! I would like some people in England to see this spectacle." From one to two thousand people began the day by attending the 7 o'clock meeting for prayer. By ten o'clock some 20,000 folk had reached the field. Processions had journeyed from Tunstall, Bradley Green, Congleton, Kidsgrove c, the Tunstall procession being 5,000 strong. The 'hymn which the parties sang on entering the field was "All hail the power of Jesus' name." From four stands, a present, full and free salvation was pro- claimed from 10.30 till 12, and from 2 till 4. In the evening Love Feasts were held in chapel and tent, while a host of the surrounding pulpits were occupied by the Centenary preachers. The weather on Sunday was not ideal ; but no drizzling rain could spoil the meaning of the great event. " Its raining hard "said one of the speakers, " so just imagine you are at a football match ;" and for once at any rate, it was proved that the national sport is not the only thing that can hold a gigantic crowd. The Soturday had opened well. Camp meetings were held from 3 to 4.30 o'clock, while indoor meetings were held at Hanley, Crewe, and Mow Cop at night. The Hanley meeting was specially memorable. Supporting the chairman, Mr. Enoch Edwards, M.P. (the Borough member) were the Mayor of Stoke, Mr. W. B. Hackney, J.P., and many of the leaders of the Primitive Methodist Church. Some half-dozen Primitive Methodist M.P.'s addressed a most enthusiastic gathering of 3,000 people. The speeches were interspersed with the heartiest of Primitive Methodist song. From C till 10 o'clock the Victoria Hall presented a most remarkable scene. It was quite evident the enthusiasm was not spent when Monday morning came. At Harecastle and Mow Cop the railway porters were full of bustle and importance. (The week-end has been like a revolution for them.) Special trains were arriving from Oldham and South Wales. How those Swansea folk did sing! It was a touch of the Welsh Revival long before we climbed the hill. The journey up was a climb that many will not readily forget. The morn- ing was ideal. The sky was full of promise for the day, the air was genial and pure. The trees and fields were looking at their very best. The lark and cuckoo were busy overhead. Those who scrambled in front and then paused to look back over a gate- way at the turn in the road, took a mental photograph of a slowly ascending and picturesque and happy- crowd. The Castle Tower still frowned above, seem- ing to say" Excelsior." When the top was reached the work at the four stands was already in full swing. Speakers and praying companies were fully engaged as though they felt that this was " the last day, that great day of the Feast." Just where to go one hardly knew, so many spiritual tables were spread. No speeches seemed too long however and a great many able speakers could be heard. From one stand came the voice of exhortation, from another the grandest of song, from yet another, the eager petitions of men in prayer, and yet though all these things might be simultaneous there seemed no interruption anywhere. The sun was now in the ascendant and the whole hill seemed bathed in heavenly glory. "Go, on" "Go on " said the responsive crowd to one who mourned the lack of time.

During the interval between the sessions the crowds dispersed to examine the places of interest with which the top of tee hill abounds. tntenng the C.JO Memorial Church the visitor might well imagmc himself in Wales. The organist was leading the congregation that streamed in and out in a number oi impromptu old-time songs. "All hail the power,"- " And can it be," "Thou Shepherd of Israel" were taken up one after another with genuine fervour and delight. From the top of the hill a most entranc- ing panorama could be seen. The Royal vale of Cheshire looked like a Paradise at our feet, while the Staffordshire side of the hill though less picturesque was even more meaningful to the Primitive Methodist mind. Ycnder was Stonebrongh Colliery, where Hugh Bourne first became impressed with the needs of the place. In front was Biddulph Moor, near at hand was the famous Bemersley, with Tunstall still a little further on. Tne interior of the Tower was crowded, and through its openings there came the sounds of heart-stirring song. Perhaps, however, the most delightful of all the gatherings of the day were the many impromptu meetings all over the hill for Chris- tian testimony and song. Here we listened to a veteran from Gateshead, who told of what the Lord had done for him, and sang

"The little cloud—increases still That first arose upon Mow Hill," and there we joined a party from Wales, who melted our hearts as they sang "Jesus, lover of my soul" to their favourite "Aberystwyth." Now we see a strong burly man almost break down with emotion, as he relates his experience and exhorts us to be filled with the Holy Ghost; and then we find ourselves catching the fire and almost longing to take part. There was not a jarring note to be heard. This was a great gathering of the clans, a religious holiday if you will, but a great thanksgiving indeed. At three o'clock the four stands were all occupied once more, College Professors,stalwart layman, earnest minister and returned missionaries were all expounding God's message to man. Bullets were flying as thick and fast as they flew at Spion Kop, but how different the day and how different the result! At each stand, a resolution against the Congo atrocities was unani- mously passed. A complete reversal of the present policy was the uncompromising lemand. About 4.30 o'clock the official open-airs were brought to a close. "Let us sing that verse over again," said the Key. T. H. Hunt, "They are coming here from the other stands." And so the song was repeated until at No. 4 stand the crowd had met en masse. "Wó are goiuB to sing thel Doxology," said the leader. "Praise God from whom all blessings flow." Did ever the listening earth hear such a rendering of the grateful verse? Did ever the listening heaven? Browning makes God say "I miss my little human praise." Here was a bit of it. God did not miss it to-day. With uncovered heads, and with greatful hearts and tear-dimmed eyes the men and women of that great crowd re-consecrated themgelve4 to God. Some of us knew our song was choked and we were not ashamed of the reason why. In the calm of the evening other out-door meetings were held, while at Mow Cop and Crewe and notably at Tunstall various indoor gatherings brought the celebrations to a close. It should he understood that the centenary, scheme is not officially launched till the Leicester Conference of 1007. For three years the connexion will be raising its centenary fund. From eleven out of the twenty-five districts three fifths of the ;E250,000 have already been promised. Liverpool District to which the local church belongs is aiming at £16,000. Our Leg,h-street friends are aiming to raise at least £2OO. Three-fifths of this will force the nucleus of a new Primitive Methodist Church and donations will be thankfully received by the Rev. C. L. Stowe or any official of the Church.

===== 0002601_19070601_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. WHITLEY'S IMPRESSIONS.

Writing on the Centenary celebrations Mr. James Whitley, Stockton Heath, says : Someone has said that Mow Cop ought to belong to the Primitive Methodists, and those who like myself were privileged to be there during the Centenary celebrations might be led to say that the Primitive Methodists had come to take possession of that rough, rugged, and pictur- esque spat. On Saturiay afternoon the great camp meeting commenced and from four stands on the Cheshire side of the hill and on the crown where the first meeting was held just 100 years ago, the Gospel was earnestly peached to the thousands who had assembled. That the mission which it was sotmlit to accomplish at the first meeting has heen fulfilled is verified bx the fact that from the first ten converts tens of thousands have been led to a better life whilst for •a century the outcasts from Society, and sinful, suffering, and down-trodden humanity have been lifted by the agency of the move- ment and led to a happier state of existence. About 4.30 pm. the meetings were brought

===== 0002601_19070601_art0122.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
_

===== 0002601_19070601_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, June 1, 1907, INFIRMARY EXTENSION.

to a close and until the hour announced for the evening service prayer meetings were held and con- ducted with great fervour. The evening meeting in the Victoria Hall, Hanley was a huge success, the greatest enthusiasm being exhibited throughout the four hours which it lasted. Several of the ten Primitive Methodist M P.'s who had been announced to speak were unavoidably prevented from being present, but the meeting was the greatest in the coarse of the celebrations and will long live in the memory as one of • the most inspiring meetings aver held in the Potteries. The hall was packed by an audience of 3,000 long before the time announced for the com- mencement of the demonstration. Mr. Enoch Edwards, M.P. for Hanley, was in the happiest possible of moods and was supported by six other M.P.'s, Messrs. Bilston, H. H. Mansfield, J. W. Taylor, L. L. Morse, W. A. Harvey and A. Richardson and a large number of principals, officials, ministers and laymen of the Connexion. A great united and well-trained choir filled the orchestra and stalls, and could and did sing, led by the magnificent organ. During the evening the Rev. T. H. Hunt introduced a deputation from the North Staffordshire Free Church Federation who congratulated the denomination upon I its prosperity and wished them every success during the celebration. Another meeting was being held at the same time in Mow Cop Chapel which was crowded and here a remarkable coincidence occurred. Lorenzo Dow the American missioner introduced the camp meetings in England exactly a century ago, whilst the first con- vert at the meetinc, on Saturday was an American to whom the writer sold a souvenir books of hymns in the afternoon. Sunday was misty and dull and the panoramic view over the Cheshire plain was obscured. The roads were blocked in all directions by immense crowds of people flocking to the camp meeting. An old inhabitant of Mow Cop told me that all pr ions meetings including the jubilee meeting were no obe compared in size with that held on Sunday, and many stated that Sunday's meetings were the largest and most enthusiastic religious gatherings ever held in the history of the world. Almost a square mile was covered with ; thronged humanity, whilst the roads were almost ;impassable. All manner of vehicles were used to con- vey the people to Mow Cop. At 6.30 in the morning I I met a fellow-townsman, who told me that he left War- rington at three o'clock and had cycled to Mow Cop to take part in the great prayer meeting at 7 a.m. Early in the morning some two or three hunired men and women assembled in the Market Square. Tunstall, and singing inspiring hymns set out upon the five-mile walk to Mow. Headed by a band they gathered strength as they proceeded on their way, until at last the procession was a mile long and some 5,000 strong. The people were well dressed and well behaved, and were evidently bent upon taking part in the religious services. Hardy- looking husbandmen and business men from Lancashire and Cheshire mingled with the less robust people from I the factories of Staffordshire, whilst miners from Durham and Northumberland joined hands with their comrades from Lancashire and Staffordshire, and testi- fied to the fact that Primitive Methodism had done much for them. The services were characterized by fervour and deep spiritual feeling. Every now and then a sentence would electity the crowd, who betrayed their Ideep emotion by exclamations of approval. There were none of the scenes of hysteria which marked the last religious revival in Wales, but there was depth of feeling and enthusiasm. It was really an old-fashioned Primitive Methodist gathering. The morning meet- ings broke up at twelve o'clock, but still thou- sands of people were flocking to Mow Cop, and by three o'clock it was estimated that between 80,000 and 100,000 persons had assembled, and the scene in the afternoon beggared description. Hundreds gathered in the Memorial Chapel to witness a unique ceremony—the christening of a centenary body—by the Rev. L. R. Hancock, the only minister ever sent out by Mow. Rain fell in the evening but it did not damp the enthusiasm. Mow Cop presented the appearance of a huge picnic but the assembly proved that an abundance of enjoyment and inspiration can be got out of life without intemper- ance. There were fears of a water famine but these was not realized as water was brought from a considerable distance in water carts. _ _

The services on Monday commenced at ten o'clock and were brought to a close when a crowd, estimatedto number 30,000,gathered round one of the preaching stands and sang the Doxology. This final scene was indescrib- able and most inspiring and affecting. A great crowd assembled at the Jubilee Church, Tunstall, in the evening when there was a great public tea followed by a meeting over which Mr. Hartley presided. The pro- ceedings were most enthusiastic, and the singing led by a large choir, very hearty. The promoters of the camp meetings were delighted with Sunday's gatherings which far exceeded their highest expectations. BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL'S FUND Meeting at Warrington.

A public meeting in support of the Bishop of Liver- pool's fund, recently instituted to raise with in the next five years a sum of at least £lOO,OOO to supply the needs of the large and increasing population of the diocese, was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, yesterday afternoon. In the absence of the Mayor who was detained on public business in London, the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis) presided and was supported by the Lord Bishop of Liverpool (Right Rev. Dr. Chavasse), Rev. Canon Sylvester (hon. secretary), Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson, Mr. J. Alderson Smith (general secretary), and Mr. J. D. Buckton. Canon Sylvester read a long list of apologies for non-attendance. The RECTOR referred to the many calls upon the generosity of church people necessitated by various pressing projects, but asked them to consider the appeal on behalf of the Bishop of Liverpool's fund on the highest possible ground. In serving the diocase they were nerving the Church of God. The Bishop had set them a most noble example by his self-denying generosity and earnest, unselfish work. (Applause). The BISHOP moved: "That the Bishop of Liver- pool's fund deserves the liberal support of all Church. people throughout the diocese." He said he had come there as Bishop not to beg but to lay before them to whom the Church belonged as much as it belonged to him, the needs of the Church in that diocese. They had received from their forefathers a great and glorious heritage and it was their bounden duty to hand it on unimpaired. The needs of the diocese were great and if their heritage were not to suffer, every member of the Church of England must bestir himself or herself. The diocese, though not very large in area, had a population of 1,300,000 and they were faced with the facts that the population was growing at the rate of 10,000 a year and that great centres of the towns were being gradually depopulated and people were flying to the suburbs, some for the sake of fresh. air and others to escape the rates. (Laughter.) Suburban parishes were therefore growing by leaps and bounds. No one could pretend to say that the funds at present at the disposal of the Biphop—£2,ooo or £3,000 a year—were adequate to meet the needs of the diocese. What was needed was a large sum which could be entrusted to a com-_--ittee and distributed, after investigation, all over the dio-rese. The object of the new fund was to advance money to build churches, mission rooms and vicarages, and enable the fit:lurch to cope with the rapidly- increasing work. Their desire was to offer to every v church that was being built £l,OOO ; every new parish hall £500; every new vicarage £ll5O ; and to help for- ward the day when every living in the diocese woe! I be at least £3OO a year. In the Winwick rural deane-y five additional clergy were needed, two parishes in Warrington which were greatly in need of larger staffs being St. Barnabss' and St. Peter's. Two churches -Hollinfare and Bartonwood—needed re-building and parish buildings were required in several parishes. Four livings at least needed bringing up to £3OO a year. One of the greatest difficulties to be faced was the migration of well-to-do people out of the diocese, leaving a vast mass of poor people often uncared for. Very often the rich people who had made their money in Lancashire towns and went to live in the beautiful parts of Cheshire forgot those through whom their money had been made. They wanted to make those people feel that the places where they had made tbeir money still had a claim upon their generosity. If rich people who were spending thonlands and thousands upon their mansions, yachts, motor cars and pleasures would give in proportion to Gad, then there would be no difficulty at all in raising £lOO.OOO. (Applause.) Colonel J. D. BUCKTON seconded and the resolution was unanimously carried. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON moved that a local com- mittee should be formed to work in conjunction with the central committee and that it should comprise one clerical and one lay narrnber frail each parish in the rural deanery. Canon SYLVESTER secondel and this resolution was also agreed to. A vote of thanks was accorded to the Rector for pre- siding, on the motion of the RIV. BR‘CEC %.MP seconded by Mr. J. ALDERSON SNIITH.

BOTHA'S TRIBUTE TO BRITAIN.

Replying to the :‘,Tayoral Welcome and an address from the Liberal Association at Capetown on Tuesday General Botha said he was convinced that the visit of the South African Premiers to England had contributed largely to a better understanding between South Africans and the people 6,000 miles away. He added that he firmly believed the British meant well by South Africa, and were encouraging every endeavour to build up a strong and united people. The Conference, he continued, would greatly contribute to the welfare of South Afrina. He was pleasurably impressed by the fact that the Colonies were regarded as sister States,and not as minor children. He was unable to go further than be did regarding preference until this important matter bad been fully and effectively discussed in the Transvaal. Alluding to thedifficulties confronting his party, especially owing to the prevalent depression, he said that they were determined not to barter their principles with any party in South Africa. South Africans, he continued, had still to learn patience, and the Transvaal Olvernment intended tl hasten slowly.

THE SUBSCRIPTIONS TO DATE. £3,000 Still Wanted.

The following letter has been issued on behalf of the'lnfirmary Extension Committee : The Infirmary and Dispensary, Warrington, June Ist, 1907. Dear Sir (or Madam), In enclosing the list of subscriptions to date the Extension Committee desire to express their keen gratification that, as the result of but little over twelve months' work, .V.. 2,000 has already been collected out of the £15,000 required. The new buildings are now nearing completion, and if the extension is to be opened on an efficient basis it is essential that it should be free from debt.

On behalf of the Colimittee we venture to earnestly appeal to all who have not yet subscribed to assist them in this work, thus aifording to the people of Warrington and surrounding districts the Infirmary accommodation essential for their requirements. Any contributions, however small, will be earnestly appreciated...

We may add that a four days' Fête and Bazaar in aid of the Fund will be held from June 19th in the grounds of Hill Cliffe, kindly lent for the purpose by Mr. Heary Greenall, and assistance in money or kind will be heartily welcomed by any of the following ladies :—Mrs. Smethurst, The Mayoress, Warrington; Mrs. Lyon, Appleton Hall ; Mrs. Henry Greenall, Hill Cliffe ; Mrs. Broadbent, Latchford ; Mrs. Blair, Moore ; Mrs. Frost, Grappenhall ; Mrs. Vere B. Davies, Stockton Heath; Mrs. Coxon, Lymm ; Mrs. Rylands, Thelwall. Yours faithfully, W. PETER RYLANDS, Chairman of the Extension Committee. GEORGE DAKIN, Hon. Treasurer. DEATH OF COLONEL W. H. DODDS4'.

Military Funeral at Newcastle.

Our rtiaders will regret to learn that Mr. James Dodds last week suffered a severe bereavement by the sudden death of his only surviving brother Colonel W. H. Dodds, V. D., at Ngwcastle-on-Tyne. The deceased gentleman was the commanding officer of the Ist Newcastle Royal Engineers (Volunteers), and had been a volunteer officer for nearly thirty years during which time he had become greatly attached to the volunteer movement and enjoyed great personal popularity. The news of his death on the Wednesday of last week was received with profound regret by all classes, and at the funeral on Saturday there was an immense gathering of people. All the officers and men of the Colonel's regiment who could possibly get away attended, and other local corps as well as regular forces were represented. The band of the regiment and the bands of the Ist Durham Engineers and the Tyne Submarine Miners accompanied the men, who would number about LOO. There were also present a large number of civic and commercial friends of Colonel Dodds in Newcastle, including the Lord Mayor, the Deputy Lord Mayor, and the Sheriff. The employees of Messrs. Johnson, Dodds and Co., of whom the deceased gentleman was managing director, also attended. The chief mourners included the sons of the deceased and Air. James Dodds and Mrs. Marshall (Bolton), the only surviving brother and sister ovt of a family of fourteen. Full military honours attended the last rites at St. Andrew's Cemetery, three volleys being fired and the " Last Post" sounded after the committal service.

At the time he received the sad news that his brother was dying Mr. James Dodds was enjoying a much-nee 'led holiday at sea. The telegram reached him at Portree, Isle of Skye, but he was unable to get toNewcastle before Col Dodds died. We are sure gr. Dodds' many friends inlWarrington will sympathise with him in his bereavement. It is a pleasure to add that he benefited by the sea-trip and that he is in much better health than he has been during the winter.

PRETTY LOCAL WEDDING.

The Runcorn Parish Church was the scene of a pretty 'wedding last week the contracting parties being Mr.. W. Simonds, of Southwood House, Atberstone, Warwickshire, and Miss Mary Wood, third daughter of Mr. Henry Wood, of Trentham-street, Runcorn. The- bride is well known in the town, and the interesting ceremony was witnessed by a large congregation. The officiating minister was the Rev. R. J. B. Morgan. The- bride, who was given away by her father, looked 'harm- ing in a dress of cream voile, trimmed with lace, surmounted by a hat of cream crinoline straw, trimmed with ostrich feathers and orange blossoms. She- also carried an exquisite shower bouquet of lilies and roses, the gift of the bridegroom. There were three bridesmaids in attendance. Miss E. Simonds (sister of the bridegroom) was prettily attired in pale heliotrope- with hat to match, and she carried a bouquet of cream roses. Miss May Wood (sister of the bride), and Miss Nellie Johnson, wore dresses of pretty delaine. with picture hats to match and each carried a basket of yellow marguerite:3 and lilies of the valley. Mr. J. Simonds, brother of the bridegroom, acted as best man. —Later in the day the happy couple left for Derby- shire, where the honeymoon will be spent •

COUNTY BOROUGH ARRINUTO N.— EDUCATION COMMITTRa• SCHOOL OF ART— HUAI:MASTER. driplications are invited for the post of Head-master of the School of Art. The remuneration is to he a fixed salary at the rate of £225 per annum. together with a grant of 10s. for each paper in excess of 125, worked hy the students of the school at the Board of Education's annual Art examinations, the total maximum salary not to exceed £3OO per annum. The successful candidate will be required to commence his duties by not later than the Ist September, 1937. Canvassing members of the Committee. personally or otherwise, will disqualify the candidate concerned. This regulation will be strictly observed. Eecretary and Director. 23rd May, 1907.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB. Monthly Prin Camintition. --- • The first of the series of five monthly prize competi- tions for the present season was held at the Range, on Saturday, when there was a very good attendance of members, most of whom competed. Altogether, 43 entries were received for the various competitions. GENERAL CONDITIONS. All distances, 100 yards.—Any military position. Any rifle, except in bronze badge class. Slings may be used, lying. A sighting shot allowed in all but disappearing target and pool bull. Below, we give the special conditions and some of the best scores made. BRONZE BADGE CLASS. Service rifles, provided by the Club, meet be used,and ammunition must be bought from the Club. Nine shots; fixed 31 inch bull, and nine shots, dis- appearing target. Possible : 90. Mr. A. Down (first prize), 58 points; Mr. J. Wilcock (second prize), 39 points ; Mr. F. J. Grocott, 32 points. The Donegall bLonze badge (provided annually by the N.R.A.) will INS awarded on the result of the shooting at the final competition, on 28th September. OPEN SWEEPSTAKES. Five shots 31 inch fixed bull. Possible, 25. Mr. P. D. Gardiner (first prize), 20 points; Mr. F. J. 1 Grocott (second prize), 19 points; Mr. R. Dennett, 18, points; Mr. J. Wilcock, 17 points; Mr. J. T. Bannion, 17 points. MEMBERS' STAKES. Seven shots, 6 inch fixed bull. Possible: 28. Mr. P. Magian (first prize), 27 points ' • Mr. F. C. Bennion (second prize), 27 points; Mr. J. Grocott 25 points; Mr. J. Wilcock 25 points; Mr. T. Daintith 21 points ; Mr. I. Bready 21 points. On a re-start between Mr. Maginn and Mr. Bennion, the former was declared the winner of the first prize. Trno rtKJs. - _ For members joined 1907 and being inexperienced, also members joined 1906 and now under 21 years of age. Seven Shots, fixed 6 inch bull. Possible: 28. As its title indicates, this competition was specially designed for the youner members of the Club, or for those who have had little experience in rifle shooting. Mr. J. A. Booth, (first prize), 24 points; Mr. P. Maginn, (second prize), 21 points; Mr. P. D. Gardiner, 23 points; Mr. F. C. Bennion, 22 points; Mr. J. Sankey, 21 points; Mr. I. Broady, 21 points; Mr. E. Plinston 21 points; Mr. E. Pollard, 21. points. Mr. Booth and Mr. Maginn having tied for the first place, a re-shoot proved Mr. Booth to be the victor. POOL BULL. Only five membess entered for this competition, which is for hits only on a 11inch bull's eye. Messrs. R. Bennett and F. C. Bennion secured one hit each, the prize being divided between them. The Club is greatly indebted to Sir Gilbert Greenall for the gift of a new War °lnce miniature rifle, which was in use for the first time in the above competitions. SCENE ON THE FOOTBALL GROUND. On Monday at St. Ilehns, a young collier named Sidney W. Jackson, of Lawrenson-street who had several bruises on his fa,ce,was charged with committing a breach of the peace. Police-constable Britch stated that on Sunday afternoon a man came to him at his house and told him there was a great fight going on on the St. Helens Rugby Football Ground in Knowsley- road. He looked through his bedroom window, which overlooked the ground, and saw two men stripped to the waist fighting in the centre of a large crowd of people. The men had seconds, towels and water. When he went on to the scene the men ran down Knowsley- road, and he chased and caught defendant. On asking forhis name and address he gave it as Peter Brown, 113, Eccleston-street, but he afterwards ascertained that this was wrong,, The Chief Constable : This was a prizefight on a Sunday afternoon. The Witness: There is no doubt about it. There is a reward of ss. offered to anyone giving information as to people trespassing on this field ?—Yes. The Magistrates Clerk :—What have you to say. about this ?—Prisoner was fighting that is all. Prisoner was ordered to find sureties for his good behavior.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Sunday School Anniversary. Special services were held at the Moore Wesleyan Church on Sunday on the occasion of the anniversary of the Sunday School. The preacher was the Rev. R. Wilkins Rees, of the Blackpool Circuit, formerly of Warrington. At the afternoon service the rev. gentle- man gave'an address to youag people on the importance of alert watchfulness, and in the evening he took as his text "She hath done what she could." Mr. Rees said there were deplorably many who through sheer want hungered for daily bread, but there were even more who at heart starved for unaccorded sympathy, help- fulness and thoughtful love. Special hymns and anthems were well rendered by the schblars and choir, under the guidance of the organist (Mr. John Banner) and the choirmaster (Mr. J. Antrobus). The offertories amounted to £l3 ss. Risley Presbyterian Church. There were large attendances at the anniversary services held in the Risley Presbotetian Church on Sunday. The morning service was conducted by Mr. John Watson, of Warrington, and the afternoon and evening service by the Rev. A. Thomas Ogilvie. Special hymns and anthems were rendered by the choir in a creditable manner, conducted by Mr. 3. Renshaw. the morning anthem being "Lift up your heads" (Belchsr), the afternoon "Let God arise" (Simper), and evening" If ye then be risen" (Simper). Messrs. W. Snailhant and E. Harrison were the soloists. In the afternoon the sacred duet "Our blest Redeemer" was creditably sung by Miss Clare and Miss Snailham. The offertories, which were in aid of the church funds, amounted to £9 17s. 6d. The Peace Conference. During the evening service at the Workingmen's Mission on Sunday the following resolution was unani- mously passed :—That this meeting of worshippers at the Workingmen's Mission Chapel, Bank Quay, Warring- ton, rejoices in the prospect of the forthcoming International Peace Conference at the Hague, and earnestly prays that its deliberations may be fruitful in promoting right understanding and mutual trust amongst the nations represented. This meeting notes with particular satisfaction that proposals towards , arresting the increase of armaments are tole introduced lat the Conference, and trusts that all who are in favour of this urgent reform will spare no effort to bring about its practical attainment. This meeting earnestly desires that the British representatives at the Confer- ence may obtain :---(1) Universal obligatory arbitration for all secondary qnwtions which do not involve Natinrial interests; (2) a Peace Budget, by which one pound will be devoted to peace propaganda and Inter- national hospitality for every £l,OOO spent on the Army and Navy. At the usual Sunday afternoon service in connection with the Emmanuel Church Men's Union held last Sunday an addres.s.was delivered by Mr. Gray on the subject of the Hague Peace Conference end was listened to with great interest by the members present. After discussion hv sofferal members it was unanimously resolved : That this meeting rejoices in the prospect of the forth- coming Intetnational Peace Conference at the Hague, and earnestly prays that its deliberations may be fruit- ful in promoting right understanding and mutual trust amongst the nations represented. This meeting notes with particular satisfaction that proposals towards arresting the increase of armaments are to be introduced at the conference, and trusts that all who are in favour of this urgent reform will spare no effort to bring about its practical attainment. This meeting earnestly desires that the British representatives at the conference may obtain : (1) Universal obligatory arbitration for all secondary questions which do not involve national interests; (2) a peace budget, by which £1 will he devoted to peace propaganda and international hospital- Ity for every £l,OOO spent on the Army and Nay.

A WARNING TO WORKMEN. Samuel Townshencl, of Clock Face, was charged at St. Helens on Tuesday, with unlawfully and maliciously breaking an electric lamp, the property of the Wigan Coal and Iron Company, doing damage to the amount of 2s. 6d. Mr. Arthur Ellis, who appeared for the prosecution, stated that the defendant was formerly in the employ of the company at their colliery, St. Helens but about three or four month ago, for certain reasons, he was dismissed. On Saturday afternoon, the 11th inst., defendant went to the colliery yard drunk. He was ordered out, but refused to go, and picking up a broom he struck at an electric lamp on the side of the wall and broke it. They had given him an opportunity on two occasions of paying the damage and expressing his regret, but he refused to do so. Defendant, who said the lamp slipped off the hook was fined ss. and 6s. costs, and ordered to pay 2s. 6d. damage. Speaking on Monday at the annnal meeting of the Children's Country Holidays Fund, in London, Sir Arthur Conan Doyle said the children of to-day were the nation of to-morrow, and if they could in any way turn the thoughts of the children to the country it might lead later in life to their finding a way into a more healthy environment. That might in after genera- tions bring an improvement in the national physique It was stated that last year 43,442 children were sent- away, or nearly 5,000 more than in 1905. CHESHIRE'S PRACTICAL COLLEGE. "Cheshire ought to feej proud of such an institution," said Mr. Balls, chairman of a deputation of Essex dairy farmers who visited the Cheshire County. Council College of Agriculture and Horticulture at Holmes Chapel on Tuesday. He added that he had never seen or heard of a more practical place.

"It is a great wonder to me," said an old chemist, "why more boys do not take up chemical experiments as an amusement. Why, I can do things with the common mate- rials of every-day life which really seem to b 3 more Magical to the uninitiated than any of the wonders that are ordinarily performed by magicians on the public stage. Some of them are BO simple, that by carrying them out at a parlour entertainment a bright boy could achieve the reputation of a magician. Now, there are those curious little bubbles of glass known variously as 'Prince Rupert's drops' and as 'Dutch tears.' Apparently they are merely little globules of glass with elongated tails made by heating a small glass rod in a flame,- and allowing the molten drops to fall into water. After they have cooled, you may pound the thick part with a hammer or mallet, yet you cannot break them. On the other hand, if you break a little piece on their tails, or toucii any part of, them with a quartz crystal, they will dig: appear into the surrounding atmosphere Quicker than snow will melt on a hot fire."
